Euler Congruences
Completion, Factorial Strengthening,
and Sharp Dyadic Geometry
Projector-Resolved Seed Calculus IX
Komorebi P.
Final strengthened edition
6 October 2026
Abstract
We prove Nishibuchi’s generalized Euler congruence conjecture in its full stated pa-
rameter range and develop sharper arithmetic consequences.
For every prime-power
packet width, a charged Hurwitz differential operator transfers integral difference estimates
through the reciprocal generating series. In the dyadic case this gives mixed finite-difference
bounds at every row, without a starting threshold. For the four addresses of width four,
a divided-power argument adds the factorial gain v2(k!), determines every repeated-step
valuation in addresses zero and one, and gives a stronger address-two estimate with exact
values at all power-of-two orders. The corresponding first differences yield least peri-
ods and bijective isometric continuations onto explicit dyadic residue classes. We also
determine exact Taylor radii for three addresses. The manuscript retains the complete
earlier arithmetic, lifting, deformation, and analytic-regularity development, with dated
historical appendices. A separate appendix states the precise remaining simplicity problem
for ordinary Euler tails. Formal coverage is explicit: the external conjecture and several
four-address results have inherited Lean verification; the new factorial, mixed-step, all-rank,
and radius arguments are written proofs with recorded audits and exact computational
checks, and await dedicated Lean formalization.
What this final edition establishes
The original external problem is Nishibuchi’s Conjecture 3.9 for generalized Euler numbers.
Its full parameter range is resolved. If p is prime, m is positive with m | p −1 or m = 2,
0 ≤j < mp, and q = lcm(2, p −1), then the original Euler coefficients are p-adically integral
and
E(mp,j)
mpn+qpr ≡E(mp,j)
mpn
(mod pr)
(r ≥1, n ≥r).
The paper states the generating function and the conversion between ordinary degree and
packet row before using this formula. The normalized coefficient is CN,j(n) and the original
coefficient is E(N,j)
Nn
= j!CN,j(n). The constant coefficient of the original series is j!.
The strongest common-step conclusion in width four is particularly compact. For n ≥0
and k, H ≥1,

There is no starting-row threshold. The exponent is exact in addresses zero and one for every
order and positive step. It is exact in address two at every power-of-two order. The proof

exact first-displacement laws.

The complete earlier Paper IX development follows alongside the strengthening. Dated
appendices retain the original continuation arguments and recovered historical text. Statements
about an earlier open target or an earlier build remain records of that checkpoint; the current
theorem and scope statements govern this edition. The Lean companion supplies the exact
formal source map and distinguishes source reconciliation from compiler execution. The visual
guide supplies exact finite examples and theorem-derived diagrams.
This is a project publication under the name Komorebi P. The recorded parallel proof
audits are internal computational-assistant reviews. They are not external peer review. The
edition makes no claim of journal acceptance, external certification, or global historical priority.
Komorebi P.

Scope and conventions
The central objects are sequences and polynomial equations equipped with arithmetic coordi-
nates. Residue projectors select addresses in a sequence, finite differences measure compatibility
between nearby addresses, and valuation bounds control the passage from finite precision to a
p-adic limit. These constructions provide a common language for the Euler packet results and
the later lifting results. Each theorem nevertheless retains its own coefficient ring, topology
and nonsingularity hypotheses.
This edition integrates the original arithmetic framework with the completed extensions.
The stated external Nishibuchi conjecture is proved in its full specified parameter range. The
dyadic refinements and polynomial deformation theorems give stronger conclusions under the
hypotheses stated with each result. The native conjectures retain their original identifiers,
13.2 and 13.3, when discussed as research questions, although the section numbering of this
consolidated manuscript is new.
Throughout, N = {0, 1, 2, . . .}. When p is prime, Zp denotes the p-adic integers and Qp
their fraction field. We normalize vp(p) = 1. For a rational or p-adic quantity, a displayed
lower valuation bound is understood with the mathematical convention vp(0) = +∞. The
corresponding formal predicates are zero-safe: the quantity is zero or its valuation meets the
stated bound. An exact finite valuation assertion requires a nonzero quantity, and this is
stated where used. Congruence in Zp means ideal divisibility. Thus
x ≡y
(mod pr)
⇐⇒
x −y ∈prZp.
For vectors, ideal membership and congruence are coordinatewise.
Write ∆uf(n) = f(n+u)−f(n) and use ∆k
u for its k-fold iterate. Euler coefficients indexed
by ordinary degree and by packet row are distinguished explicitly. A packet displacement by u
rows is a degree displacement by Nu in a packet of width N.
The main text presents theorem statements and their arguments; accompanying source
maps identify the Lean declarations and archived files that support them. The verification
record keeps the original dates and scope of each build. In particular, the latest 91-check
polynomial build covers the polynomial development, while earlier builds support the arithmetic
results. The inherited formal development is retained without adding Lean declarations. The
strengthening chapters add written proofs and clearly mark their formalization boundary; no
historical-priority claim is made. The analytic inverse-function results used in Section 11 come
from Mathlib.
Komorebi P.

2
Arithmetic packets and coefficient transport
We work with sequences, formal power series, and finite recurrence states. Residue selectors
isolate coefficients in a chosen congruence class, while triangular transport expresses the same
object in new coordinates. A valuation filtration determines how many coordinates are needed
at a given precision. Together, these operations provide a common framework for the Euler
congruences and the finite lifting constructions developed later.
The original arithmetic seed notation
S = (A, a0, O, R, {PN,j}, w, τp)
records a coefficient ring or module, a seed, allowed operators, relations, residue selectors, a
depth weight, and a topology. This notation describes the ingredients of the construction; a
category of seed presentations and a notion of equivalence still require separate definitions.
We therefore state each theorem for a concrete object. Section 12 discusses the additional
structure needed for the original native conjectures.
The Lean declarations cited in this section have namespace prefix KomorebiPaperIX. Their
exact modules and source hashes are recorded in the accompanying statement map. All
belong to the preserved baseline formalization; this section brings their statements and proofs
together.
2.1
Residue selectors and exact reconstruction
Let R be a commutative ring, a : N →R, N ≥1, and 0 ≤j < N. Define
(PN,ja)(n) =

This definition keeps the original index set. The compressed subsequence a(Nq + j), used
below for recurrence packets, is a different representation of the selected coefficients.
Theorem 2.1 (Residue reconstruction). The selectors satisfy
P 2

The same identities hold coefficientwise for formal power series.
Proof. At each index n, exactly one standard residue label equals n mod N. Consequently a
repeated selector changes nothing, two different selectors have zero composite, and the sum
retains a(n) exactly once. If N | M, reducing n mod M modulo N gives n mod N. This proves
the refinement identity at every coefficient. Equality of all coefficients proves the formal-series
versions.
Formal correspondence. ResiduePackets.packet_idempotent, ResiduePackets.packet_orthogonal,
ResiduePackets.packet_reconstruction, and ResiduePackets.packet_refinement; the series variants
have the prefix series_packet in the same module. The algebraic statements need no convergence
hypothesis.

Theorem 2.9 (Finite unit coordinates). Let p be prime, L ≥0, and a ∈(Z/pL+1Z)×. Define
ωL(a) = apL,
⟨a⟩L = aωL(a)−1.
Then
a = ωL(a)⟨a⟩L,
ωL(a)p−1 = 1,
⟨a⟩L ≡1
(mod p).
The two factors are uniquely determined by these properties and are compatible with reduction
to lower precision. For a ∈(Z/2L+2Z)×, there is instead a unique decomposition
a = ϵ(a)⟨a⟩4,
ϵ(a) ∈{1, −1},
⟨a⟩4 ≡1
(mod 4).
Proof. The unit-group exponent pL(p −1) gives the root identity for ωL. Its residue is that
of a, so the remaining factor has residue 1. If two (p −1)st roots have equal residue, raising
them to pL makes them equal modulo pL+1, while each root is fixed by this exponent. This
proves uniqueness and then compatibility under reduction. In the dyadic construction, an
odd residue is exactly 1 or −1 modulo 4. Choose that sign and multiply a by it to obtain the
principal factor. The two signs remain distinct modulo 4, which proves uniqueness.
Formal correspondence. FiniteUnits.finite_unit_decomposition, FiniteUnits.decomposition_unique,
UnitRefinement.omega_compatible, UnitRefinement.principal_compatible, and
DyadicUnits.dyadic_unit_decomposition with DyadicUnits.decomposition_unique. The finite p = 2
version of the first construction has trivial root-of-unity factor. The separate modulo-4 construction
retains the sign that reduction modulo 2 cannot detect.

Proof. The jump identity follows from the power law for one matrix. Apply the Cayley–
Hamilton identity χCN (CN) = 0 to the state bk. The leading coefficient is 1, so the final state

Formal correspondence. Companion.companion_jump, Companion.packet_charpoly_recurrence, and

Theorem 2.11 (Dyadic–odd packet factorization). Let a ≥0 and let M be a positive odd
integer. The Chinese remainder map is a ring isomorphism
Z/(2aM)Z ≃Z/2aZ × Z/MZ.
For 0 ≤r < 2aM and any coefficient sequence f,
P2aM,rf = P2a,r mod 2a PM,r mod Mf.
The same identity holds for formal-series coefficient selectors.
Proof. The two moduli are coprime. An index has the prescribed combined residue exactly
when it has both component residues. Applying this equivalence to the indicator defining each
selector proves the formula.
Formal correspondence. CRTPackets.addressEquiv, CRTPackets.residue_equivalence,
CRTPackets.packet_factorization, and CRTPackets.series_packet_factorization. This theorem
synchronizes finite arithmetic addresses. The passage from a family of p-adic objects to a global integer
object is a separate question, discussed in Section 12.3.

3
Congruential Euler packets and their arithmetic
Two related constructions run through this section. The congruential Euler family is indexed by
a modulus N and an address j. Its exceptional dyadic member has N = 4 and four addresses.
The classical signed Euler numbers arise from N = 2, j = 0. Keeping these indices visible
distinguishes the four-packet period theorems from the classical edge truncations.

even coefficients yields
C2,1(n) = (2 −22n)B2n
(n ∈N).
This classical derivation restores the boundary example from the original manuscript. No
dedicated Lean bridge for this Bernoulli specialization was identified in the preserved sources.
Definition 3.4 (Differences and valuation conventions). For a sequence f and h, k, n ∈N, put
∆hf(n) = f(n + h) −f(n),
∆0
hf(n) = f(n),
∆k+1
h
f(n) = ∆k
hf(n + h) −∆k
hf(n).
We use the extended valuation vp(0) = +∞in mathematical statements. Thus vp(x) ≥L,
including negative integers L, means x = 0 or the ordinary valuation of the nonzero rational x
is at least L. For integral coefficients and L ≥0, it is equivalent to x ∈pLZp. In expressions
involving a step multiplier we write νp(u) = vp(u) for u > 0 and set νp(0) = 0. This finite
convention makes the displayed exponents meaningful at step zero, where all positive-order
differences vanish.
The valuation convention at zero is encoded by KomorebiPaperIX.GlobalEulerBoundary.precis
ion. For p-adic congruences, divisibility is interpreted in Zp; statements over other coefficient
rings specify the ring explicitly. Field divisibility in Qp alone does not encode precision.
3.2
The standard address window and its common reduction
The addresses below the prime have unit diagonal coefficients. In this window the normalized
packets are integral, and their reductions agree at every row.
Proposition 3.5 (Lucas reduction and address independence). Let p be prime, m > 0, and
N = mp. For 0 ≤j < p and n, q ∈N,

Consequently, for any two addresses 0 ≤j, k < p,

Proof. Lucas reduction separates the lowest base-p digits, which are j and zero in the upper
and lower arguments. Their binomial contribution is one, leaving the displayed reduced row.
On the diagonal the reduced coefficient is one. The recurrence (3.3) therefore constructs an
integral packet by successive division by units in Zp. After reduction, every address satisfies
the same triangular system over Fp. Its diagonal entries are one, so its solution is unique.
This proves both the common reduction and the congruence between addresses. The row
reduction is KomorebiPaperIX.StandardWindow.lucas_row_reduction. The integral identification and
the two reduction statements are KomorebiPaperIX.WindowIndependence.standard_window_integral,
KomorebiPaperIX.WindowIndependence.coeffPadic_coe, KomorebiPaperIX.WindowIndependence.residue

3.3
The full Nishibuchi statement and its starting bound
Theorem 3.6 (Nishibuchi congruence). Let p be prime, let m > 0, and suppose that m | p −1
or m = 2. Put N = mp and q = lcm(2, p −1). For every 0 ≤j < N, all UN,j(n) belong to Zp.
For r ≥1 and n ≥r,

Thus the positive sufficient starting index n0 = r works uniformly. If m | p −1, the stronger
sufficient condition r ≤mn is valid.
This theorem covers the full parameter range and displacement of Conjecture 3.9 in the
fixed version of Nishibuchi’s paper used for the statement comparison. The corresponding
Lean theorem is KomorebiPaperIX.NishibuchiCompletion.conjecture_3_9. The stronger divisor-
branch threshold is KomorebiPaperIX.NishibuchiCompletion.divisor_period. The starting index is
sufficient uniformly, although it need not be the earliest one for a given tuple of parameters.
We first prove the difference estimates on the divisor branch, then derive the period from a
finite Newton expansion.

including its zero coefficients off the packet support, require a separate analysis. The new
strengthening chapters determine all repeated-step higher differences in addresses zero and
one and a power-of-two-order family in address two. The full exact classification of arbitrary
higher-order differences are also a separate question from the first-displacement law.

3.8
Isometric continuation of the four dyadic packets
The exact displacement law also determines the images of the packets after continuous extension
to the dyadic integers.
Komorebi P.

Theorem 3.25 (Isometric packet bijections). For 0 ≤j < 4, define on natural numbers
fj(n) = Uj(n) −j!
2dj
,
(d0, d1, d2, d3) = (1, 1, 5, 3).
There is a unique continuous map Φj : Z2 →Z2 whose value at every n ∈N equals the
embedded rational fj(n). It is an isometric bijection and Φj(0) = 0:
|Φj(x) −Φj(y)|2 = |x −y|2
(x, y ∈Z2).
In particular, every element of Z2 has exactly one preimage.
Proof. For n > 0, the displacement law at row zero gives v2(Uj(n) −j!) = v2(n) + dj, so
fj(n) ∈Z2. The value at zero is zero. Applying the same law to the smaller of two unequal
natural numbers shows
|fj(n) −fj(m)|2 = |n −m|2.
The natural numbers are dense in Z2, and the target is complete. The map therefore extends
continuously. The distance identity extends on the dense product of natural-number points,
making the extension an isometry. Density also proves uniqueness among all continuous maps
with these prescribed values.
To prove surjectivity, use compactness. For an isometry g of a compact metric space into
itself, the sequence of iterates gn(x) has a convergent subsequence. Two sufficiently close
iterates, with exponents a < b, yield d(x, gb−a(x)) equally small after removing a isometric
iterations. Since b −a > 0, the latter point is in the image of g. Thus the image is dense. It is
also compact and hence closed, so it is the whole space. Apply this to Φj on Z2.
The general ingredients are KomorebiPaperIX.DensePadicIsometry.existsUnique_extensionEquiv
and KomorebiPaperIX.CompactSelfIsometry.surjective.
The packet specialization is Komore
biPaperIX.DyadicFourIsometry.existsUnique_isometry; uniqueness among continuous maps is
KomorebiPaperIX.DyadicFourIsometry.unique_continuous.
Corollary 3.26 (Exact image of the original coefficients). The continuous extension of the
original sequence is
eUj(x) = j! + 2djΦj(x).
It is injective, its image is exactly j! + 2djZ2, and
| eUj(x) −eUj(y)|2 = 2−dj|x −y|2.
Every point of that affine image has exactly one preimage in Z2.
Proof. The formula agrees with Uj on every natural number. Continuity and the distance
equality follow from the corresponding properties of Φj. Its surjectivity gives precisely the
displayed affine image, and multiplication by the nonzero scalar 2dj preserves injectivity. The
declarations are KomorebiPaperIX.DyadicFourIsometry.originalExtension_nat, KomorebiPaperIX.Dyad
icFourIsometry.originalExtension_dist, and KomorebiPaperIX.DyadicFourIsometry.originalExtensi
on_range.
The four rational packet sequences thus extend to bijections onto their exact dyadic images.
The proof uses density, completeness, and the displacement law. The strengthening chapters
supply the further factorial and Taylor-coefficient argument for analyticity, including exact
radii in addresses zero, one and two. The analytic polynomial solution maps constructed later
rest on separate hypotheses and a separate proof.

4
Threshold-free reciprocal transfer at prime-power section
orders
The completion of Nishibuchi’s conjecture fixes the requested eventual congruence. The stronger
question is whether the sufficient starting row can be removed, and how much precision the
difference order and the step actually provide. This section answers the first question at every
prime-power section order. Subsequent sections give a factorial improvement and exact results
at section order four.
Throughout these sections, the normalized source and its reciprocal are

starting rows are nonnegative integers; steps are positive integers unless stated otherwise. An
empty mixed difference is the original value, and a zero ordinary step makes every nonempty
product containing it zero.

Remark 4.6 (Necessary boundaries). At an odd prime the ordinary step-one difference cannot
replace the sign-twisted operator in the general theorem.
For j = 0, CN,0(0) = 1 and
CN,0(1) = −1, so their ordinary difference is −2, a unit at every odd prime. The proof also
does not extend unchanged to non-prime-power N, whose interior binomial coefficients need
not be divisible by the chosen prime. Neither observation is a counterexample to Nishibuchi’s
original eventual congruence.
5
Factorial precision at section order four
Write Cj = C4,j and Fj = F4,j. The all-row theorem removes the threshold, but at N = 4 it
leaves a further factorial gain unused. The exact algebra below proves that gain for all four
addresses and identifies several infinite families where it is sharp.
Theorem 5.1 (Four-address factorial bound). For j ∈{0, 1, 2, 3}, n ≥0, k ≥1, and positive

The proof of the first two addresses uses one classical arithmetic input: Frobenius’s exact
congruence for the ordinary Euler numbers in the sech convention, as stated and derived by
Gessel [2], Section 9. The address-two and address-three bounds below are finite Bernoulli
calculations and do not require that input. A divided-power argument is necessary: ordinary
integral moments alone do not justify division by k!.

6
Sharp higher-difference families
The lower bounds do not mean that every mixed stencil has exactly the stated valuation.
Addresses zero and one have a complete classification of when the leading factorial precision is
attained. Address two has exact mixed precision at every positive power-of-two order.
Theorem 6.1 (Exact factorial precision at addresses zero and one). Let j ∈{0, 1}, k ≥1,
n ≥0, and H1, . . . , Hk > 0. Put
L = k + v2(k!) +
k
X
ν=1
v2(Hν),
e = #{ν : 2 | Hν}.
The mixed difference has valuation exactly L if k is odd or e is even. If k is even and e is odd,
it has valuation at least L + 1. In particular, for every homogeneous positive step,
v2(∆k
HCj(n)) = k + v2(k!) + kv2(H).
(6.1)
Proof. Return to the Euler variables of address zero and put X = P + Q, τ = iEF, ϵ = 1 + τ.
Direct rearrangement of (5.6) gives
H0 = γ2(X)+ϵX+2G,
G = −γ2(P)−γ2(Q)+PQ−τQ,
ϵ2 = 2(τ−P−Q−2PQ). (6.2)
The divided powers of G are integral by the same component formulas used above. Thus
modulo 2A0, γk(H0) equals the divided power of γ2(X) + ϵX. The identity
γr(γ2(X)) = (2r)!
2rr! γ2r(X)

39


Euler congruences / Paper IX
Final strengthened edition
has an odd integer scalar. Every term involving ϵj for j ≥2 vanishes modulo two. The
surviving zero and first powers give
γk(H0) ≡γ2k(X) + ϵγ2k−1(X)
(mod 2A0).
(6.3)
The multiplication coefficient in the second term is 2k −1, also odd. By the odd moments
(5.4) and independence,
L(γm(P + Q)) ≡m + 1
(mod 2).
The τ-weighted moment vanishes because it contains an odd power of each Euler variable.
Therefore the first term of (6.3) evaluates to one modulo two and the second to zero. Hence
L(γk(H0)) is odd at every order, including order zero. This proves
v2(∆k
1C0(0)) = k + v2(k!).
In the base-row expansion (5.9), every s > 0 term has at least one extra bit after division by
2kk!. Thus the same exact valuation holds at every starting row. The extra-bit transfer (5.19)
proves it for address one as well.
It remains to track possible ties in the mixed-step expansion. For local orders mν ≥1 and
total M, the excess over L is at least
 
M −k −
X

v2(mν)

+ (v2(M!) −v2(k!)) .

The first bracket is nonnegative, and equality requires every mν to be one or two. Two or more
occurrences of two make the second bracket positive, since the intervening factorial interval
then contains an even number. Exactly one occurrence of two can tie only when k is even;
its binomial coefficient can have the minimally allowed valuation only if its associated step is
even.
The all-one multi-index contributes one after normalization by 2L. When k is even, each
even step supplies exactly one further tied term, again with normalized residue one. For
example,
 H

and the exact normalized order-k + 1 difference are odd. Thus the total normalized parity is
one for odd k, and 1 + e for even k. This proves the classification. In the homogeneous case, e
is zero or k, so it is even whenever k is even, proving (6.1).
Remark 6.2 (A mixed cancellation). The exceptional class can gain more than one bit. The

A total-order-three correction loses at most one bit, so it stays above the leading valuation
six, while later orders gain still more. The same argument proves exactness for both mixed
steps.
Remark 6.4 (What the power orders do not decide). The exact orders in Theorem 6.3 form an
infinite family. They do not supply a complete valuation formula at non-power-of-two orders
in address two, nor an exact higher-difference formula at address three. Numerical patterns at
other orders remain bounded observations unless proved separately.

7
Exact first differences, least periods, and dyadic images
The factorial estimates are stronger than needed for the optimal first-difference law. A general
Newton criterion gives the exact law from the threshold-free bound and finitely many rational
coefficients. This route also yields all addresses at ranks two and eight, and an infinite family
at every larger dyadic rank.
Lemma 7.1 (A first-difference criterion). Let C : Z≥0 →Z2 have Newton coefficients
ak = ∆k
1C(0). Suppose for an integer b ≥0 that
v2(a1) = b,
v2(ak) ≥b + 1 + ⌊log2 k⌋
(k ≥2).
(7.1)
Then for every n ≥0 and H > 0,
v2(C(n + H) −C(n)) = b + v2(H).
(7.2)
The Newton interpolation extends continuously to Z2 and satisfies the same identity for any
two distinct dyadic arguments.
Proof. At a natural argument the exact Newton expansion is finite. Vandermonde gives
 
n + H
k
!
−
 
n
k
!
=
k
X
i=1
 
H
i
! 
n

For 1 ≤i ≤H, v2(
 H

each Newton summand with k ≥2 has valuation at least b + v2(H) + 1. The k = 1 summand
is Ha1, of exact valuation b + v2(H), proving (7.2).
The coefficient hypothesis implies ak →0 dyadically, so the Newton series converges
uniformly on Z2, where
 x
k
 is integral-valued. For distinct x, y ∈Z2, approximate the pair
by natural integer pairs with their difference valuation eventually equal to v2(x −y). The
corresponding function differences have the fixed exact valuation b + v2(x −y). A limit of
elements with that fixed valuation has the same valuation: the normalized residues are units,
and Z×

Proof. The exact difference identity gives v2(Tj(x) −Tj(y)) = v2(x −y) and Tj(0) = 0. For
every s ≥1, the map induces an injection of Z2/2sZ2 into itself. It is a bijection because both
sets have 2s elements. These quotient bijections are compatible with reduction. Inverting them
gives a compatible preimage system for every dyadic integer, proving surjectivity. Multiplication
by j! changes the image ideal by exactly v2(j!). The finite quotient bijection is also precisely
the residue enumeration in a least row period.

8
Analytic interpolation and exact Taylor radii
The threshold-free Mahler theorem gives continuous interpolation. Analytic interpolation
needs a stronger estimate, and the factorial bounds provide it directly. The ordinary Taylor
radius is determined by the coefficient growth, including infinite exact subsequences.

Remark 8.2 (Boundary interpretation). For addresses zero and one, at an argument of absolute
value two every positive-degree Taylor term has valuation zero, so that ordinary Taylor series
diverges. For address two, over a complete extension admitting arguments of absolute value
25/2, the power-of-two-degree terms have valuation zero and fail to tend to zero. These
statements concern the Taylor expansions just constructed; they do not prohibit a different
analytic continuation. On Q2-valued arguments, open-disc notation must be read with its
discrete value group. The coefficient radius is the precise invariant in the theorem.
9
The final congruence in the original Euler convention
The preceding bounds can be stated as one uniform congruence without suppressing the
address normalization. This is the strengthened formula for the four-address family, valid from
the first row.

9.1
Proof coverage and remaining questions
The results in these sections are mathematical theorems with detailed proofs and preserved
audit evidence. Their formal status must be read statement by statement. The recovered
Paper IX release already contains Lean results for the rank-four threshold-free homogeneous
bound, the sharp first-difference laws, the least periods, and the dyadic isometry and image
conclusions. Those inherited results should not be described as missing merely because the
earlier short completion supplement did not include their later modules.
The all-rank dyadic and general prime-power transfer proof presented here, the factorial
improvements, the mixed leading-parity classification, the Bernoulli power-order sharpness, the
all-rank lower-half exact laws, the complete rank-eight extension, and the exact Taylor-radius
arguments have not received new Lean formalization in this strengthening. A mathematical
audit and an exact computation are neither a Lean compilation nor external peer review. The
separate Lean guide records the recovered formal declarations and any fresh release checks
without extending their coverage to these new claims.
The completed external statement is Nishibuchi’s Conjecture 3.9 in its prescribed parameter
family and coefficient normalization. These stronger congruences enlarge its dyadic branch
and remove its starting threshold there. They do not settle every Euler problem. In particular,
the following questions remain separate:
• a complete exact higher-difference law at all non-power-of-two orders in address two;
• complete higher-difference valuations and the exact ordinary Taylor radius in address
three;
• a classification of the upper-half dyadic addresses having an exact first-difference metric
law at arbitrary larger ranks;
• the distinct universal simplicity or nonvanishing question for the later Euler-tail residuals,
and the original native Paper IX conjectures beyond the completed external statement.
The exact counterexamples above rule out the naive universal upper-half isometry, and mixed
cancellations rule out unconditional baseline sharpness for arbitrary stencils.
No global
historical-priority or novelty assertion is made for these strengthened statements.
Komorebi P.
47


Euler congruences / Paper IX
Final strengthened edition
10
Finite obstructions, compatible lifts, and deformation
The arithmetic results above concern particular families.
We now study the underlying
correction problem, from finite polynomial lifts to continuous parameter charts and quantitative
deformation. Throughout, we distinguish three objects: a residual obstruction, the set of
correcting digits, and a root in a completion. A vanishing obstruction at one precision gives a
lift to the next. Continuing indefinitely requires additional hypotheses.
Unless a different coefficient ring is specified, a polynomial system is a finite list of integer
polynomials. All divisibility conditions on vectors are coordinatewise. For a positive integer q,
write Sq = Z/qZ and use an overline for reduction modulo q. For a prime p, write Fp = Sp,
normalize |p|p = p−1, and put
BL(x) = x + pLZd
p.
An integer center is included in this notation by its natural image in Zd
p. All dimensions below
are finite and may be zero. A statement over Sq allows composite q and q = 1 when explicitly
stated. A cardinality formula in terms of vector-space rank uses prime p.
10.1
The polynomial correction equation
For F = (F1, . . . , Fe) ∈Z[X1, . . . , Xd]e, define its evaluated Jacobian by the formal partial
derivatives:
DF(x)ij = (∂jFi)(x),
Jx = DF(x) mod q : Sd
q −→Se
q.
The obstruction therefore uses the Jacobian of the given polynomials. The following algebraic
Taylor identity relates this linear map to polynomial evaluation.
Lemma 10.1 (Polynomial Taylor remainder). Let R be a commutative ring, P

In particular, when R = Z and a, u, c are integral, subtracting the first two terms on the right
from P(a + cu) gives a multiple of c2.
Proof. Apply polynomial induction using constants, addition, and multiplication by a variable.
Constants have zero remainder, and remainders add. For the multiplication step, suppose the
assertion is known for P and write DP (U) = P

Proposition 10.3 (Affine depth zero). Let F(x) = Ax −b for an integer linear map A : Zd →

The affine statement is formalized by Komorebi.NativeAffine.finite_affine_obstruction.
Positive depth is essential in the nonlinear theorem: for F(X) = X2 −1, q = 2, L = 0, and

the same residue modulo one.
Proposition 10.4 (Independence of the integer representative). Under the hypotheses of
Theorem 10.2, let x′ = x + qLs and write F(x′) = qLrx′. Then
Jx′ = Jx,
rx′ = rx + Jxs.
Consequently the two residual classes coincide in the common quotient Se
q/ im Jx.
Proof. Polynomial evaluation respects coordinatewise congruence. Apply this first to every
formal partial derivative: x′ ≡x (mod q) gives Jx′ = Jx. Next apply (10.1) to F at scale
qL. Subtract the two exact residual identities and cancel qL to obtain the displayed relation
modulo q. Its additional term belongs to the common Jacobian image, so it vanishes in the
quotient.
This comparison is formalized by Komorebi.PolynomialRepresentative.representative_indep
endence. This proves independence of the integer representative within a fixed polynomial
presentation. It makes no identification of the raw cokernels of different equation lists. For
example, adjoining a zero equation leaves the roots unchanged while adding an Sq summand
to the raw cokernel.

10.2
One correction shared by two systems
The next obstruction concerns compatibility between individually attainable corrections. Its
algebraic form works over an arbitrary commutative ring. Let U, V, W be R-modules and let
J : U →V , K : U →W be linear. Define
C(J, K) = U/(ker J + ker K).
(10.4)
An attainable value Ju has label [u] ∈C(J, K); this is independent of the choice of its preimage
because ker J is killed in the quotient. Similarly, Kv has label [v]. Denote these maps by
λJ : im J →C(J, K) and λK : im K →C(J, K).
Theorem 10.5 (Common correction and fiber product). For every u, v ∈U,
[u] = [v] in C(J, K)
⇐⇒
∃w ∈U : Jw = Ju,
Kw = Kv.
(10.5)
Both label maps are surjective, and the image of the joint map is exactly the fiber product:
im
 U →im J × im K, u 7→(Ju, Ku)
 = im J ×C(J,K) im K.
(10.6)
If either V or W is finite, then C(J, K) is finite.
Proof. If [u] = [v], write u −v = s + t with s ∈ker J and t ∈ker K. Then w = u −s = v + t
satisfies both required equalities. Conversely, a common w gives
u −v = (u −w) + (w −v) ∈ker J + ker K.
Every class [u] is the label of Ju and of Ku, proving surjectivity. For attainable a = Ju and
b = Kv, the first equivalence says exactly that equal labels are equivalent to a simultaneous
preimage. This proves (10.6). Finally, a finite target has finite image, and the associated
surjective label map makes C(J, K) finite.
These statements are Komorebi.SharedCorrection.quotient_mk_eq_iff_common and Komorebi.S
haredCorrection.joint_range_eq_fiber, together with the finite-target corollaries in the same
module. The quotient also has the expected universal property: every linear map from U
annihilating both kernels factors uniquely through C(J, K). Equivalently, compatible linear
observations on im J and im K factor uniquely through their label maps. This is formalized
by Komorebi.SharedCorrection.common_paired_universal.
Corollary 10.6 (Simultaneous polynomial lifting). Let F ∈Z[X1, . . . , Xd]e and G ∈
Z[X1, . . . , Xd]f have a common lower root x modulo qL, where q ≥1 and L ≥1. Use the
integer-domain reduced Jacobians J : Zd →Se
q and K : Zd →Sf
q . Suppose u, v ∈Zd are
separately valid corrections for F and G, respectively. Then
[u] = [v] in Zd/(ker J + ker K)
if and only if a single y ≡x (mod qL) solves both systems modulo qL+1. Each separate label is
independent of the chosen valid correction. The common quotient is finite.
Proof. For each system, (10.3) translates a valid next root into a linear correction equation.
Once u corrects F, another digit w corrects F precisely when Jw = Ju. The analogous
condition for G is Kw = Kv. Theorem 10.5 now gives the criterion. Two valid corrections of
one block differ by a vector in its Jacobian kernel, so they have equal labels. Finiteness follows
from either finite residue target. An unconditional formulation quantifies over separately valid
u, v with equal labels, thereby including their existence in the criterion rather than assuming
it.
Komorebi P.
50


Euler congruences / Paper IX
Final strengthened edition
See Komorebi.PolynomialShared.joint_lift_iff_class_eq and Komorebi.PolynomialShared.finite
_polynomial_pair_compatibility.
For affine integer systems F(X) = AX −b and G(X) = BX −c, where A : Zd →Ze
and B : Zd →Zf are linear, Corollary 10.6 holds for q ≥1 and every L ≥0, including
depth zero. Here x is a common root modulo qL, and J and K are the reductions of A
and B with domain Zd. Given separately valid corrections u, v, a simultaneous next lift
exists if and only if their classes in Zd/(ker J + ker K) agree. The quotient is finite, and
each label is independent of the chosen valid correction. Indeed, Proposition 10.3 supplies
the correction equation without a positive-depth restriction, and the proof of Theorem 10.5
then applies unchanged. The formal statement is Komorebi.AffinePair.joint_lift_iff_class_eq.
Quantifying over separately valid corrections with equal labels also includes their existence in
the criterion, as in Komorebi.AffinePair.finite_affine_pair_compatibility. Separate liftability
alone is insufficient. At x = 0 modulo two, F(X) = X and G(X) = X −2 both lift to modulus
four, but their correcting digits are respectively 0 and 1 modulo two. No single residue solves
both equations modulo four.
10.3
Correction torsors and exact residue counts
Integer representatives and residue classes at the next level must also be distinguished. For
q ≥1 and L ≥0, the map
Sd
q −→{y ∈(Z/qL+1Z)d : y ≡x
(mod qL)},
t 7−→x + qLet
is a bijection, where et is any integer representative. Independence of the representative and
injectivity both follow by cancelling qL in integer divisibility; surjectivity follows by expressing
the lower congruence as a multiple of qL. This is the equivalence Komorebi.ResidueLiftDigits.d
igitEquivResidue.
At positive polynomial depth, restrict it to the correction fiber

The polynomial predicate in the second display is evaluation in the residue ring. Evaluation
on canonical integer representatives gives the same predicate, because integer polynomial
evaluation respects congruence.
Theorem 10.7 (Torsor and lift cardinalities). Under the hypotheses of Theorem 10.2, there
is an equivalence Tx ≃Lx,L. Every nonempty fiber is a torsor for ker Jx under addition of

0,

Proof. Equation (10.3) identifies exactly which points of the residue fiber satisfy the equations,
proving the restricted equivalence. If u, t ∈Tx, subtraction gives Jx(t −u) = 0. Conversely,
adding any element of ker Jx preserves the correction equation. The unique kernel vector
carrying u to t is t −u, so the action is free and transitive. Choosing one valid digit identifies
the fiber with the kernel, without making that choice part of its translation action.
For prime p, rank-nullity gives dim ker Jx = d −ρx and dim(Fe
p/ im Jx) = e −ρx. The
cardinality of an n-dimensional Fp-space is pn. Combine these identities with the obstruction
criterion and the residue equivalence.

The cardinality formula is formalized by Komorebi.PolynomialLiftCounts.finite_polynomia

For composite q, the torsor and kernel cardinality remain valid, while the vector-space rank
formula is unavailable. Multiplication by two on Z/4Z already has a kernel of size two.
10.4
Smooth compatible towers and exact roots
Surjectivity removes every residual obstruction and persists throughout a positive-depth residue
ball. This supplies the additional hypothesis needed to iterate the finite theorem.
Theorem 10.8 (Compatible polynomial tower). Let q ≥1, L ≥1, F ∈Z[X1, . . . , Xd]e, and
x ∈Zd satisfy F(x) ∈qLZe. Suppose Jx : Sd
q →Se
q is surjective. There is a single sequence
zn ∈Zd such that
z0 = x,
F(zn) ∈qL+nZe,
zn+1 −zn ∈qL+nZd,
Jzn = Jx.
For a ≤b, it also satisfies zb −za ∈qL+aZd.
Proof. At level N = L + n, divide the residual by qN. Surjectivity supplies t ∈Sd
q with
Jznt = −F(zn)/qN. Choose an integer representative and apply (10.3) to obtain zn+1. The
new point is congruent to zn modulo q. Evaluation of each formal partial derivative therefore
gives Jzn+1 = Jzn.
Construct the sequence recursively, retaining the root condition and the fixed Jacobian at
each step. Surjectivity at every later stage then follows from the single initial assumption. The
cross-level congruence follows by induction, adding consecutive differences whose divisibilities
all imply divisibility by qL+a.
The full tower statement is Komorebi.PolynomialIntegerTower.exists_compatible_tower_strong.
The proof constructs a compatible path using classical choice. It does not claim an optimized
procedure for selecting its digits.
Theorem 10.9 (Completion and smooth branching). In Theorem 10.8, let q = p be prime.
The tower can be chosen with a limit a ∈Zd
p such that
F(a) = 0,
zn −→a,
a −zn ∈pL+nZd
p
(n ≥0).
At each stage, the number of distinct next-level residue roots over zn is exactly pd−e, and the
finite obstruction module has one element. If d = e, there is exactly one root of F in BL(x).
Proof. Cross-level compatibility bounds the supremum distance between later terms by p−(L+n).
Completeness gives a limit, and closed norm bounds retain the congruence to every zn. View
the coefficients of the original integer polynomials in Zp. Their evaluations satisfy both
Fi(zn) →Fi(a), by continuity, and Fi(zn) →0, by their growing divisibility. Uniqueness of
limits gives exact vanishing.
Surjectivity gives rank Jx = e ≤d. Theorem 10.7 then gives pd−e next residue roots and
obstruction cardinality one at every stage.
For square uniqueness, let a, b ∈BL(x) be arbitrary exact roots. Their canonical integer
truncations are genuine roots in the respective finite residue rings, since evaluation commutes
with reduction. They agree at depth L. If they agree at depth N ≥L, both next residues
Komorebi P.
52


Euler congruences / Paper IX
Final strengthened edition
belong to the same lift set, which has cardinality pd−d = 1. Induction gives agreement at every
depth. The residue maps separate p-adic integers, so a = b. Existence was already supplied by
the tower.
The endpoint construction is Komorebi.PolynomialPadicLift.exists_padic_root_with_tower, the
branching statement is Komorebi.PolynomialSmoothCounts.exists_regular_tower, and uniqueness
for all roots in the ball is Komorebi.PolynomialPadicUnique.exists_unique_padic_root.
Remark 10.10. The one-step singular criterion and the smooth infinite theorem have different
hypotheses. The polynomial X2 −5 has odd roots modulo two and four but has no root
modulo eight. Thus a previous zero obstruction does not force the next one to vanish. Also,
rectangular surjectivity alone does not imply uniqueness: F(X, Y ) = X has the distinct dyadic
roots (0, 0) and (0, 2) in the same residue ball modulo two. These examples show why the
hypotheses of the tower theorem and its uniqueness clause are needed.
10.5
Coefficient stability and continuous root charts
Proposition 10.11 (Smooth coefficient stability). Let p be prime, M
≥1, F, H ∈
Z[X1, . . . , Xd]e, and c ∈pMZ. Suppose DF(x) mod p is surjective for an integer x ∈Zd. If
a ∈Zd
p satisfies F(a) = 0 and a ≡x (mod p), then there is a root b of G = F + cH with
b −a ∈pMZd
p.
If d = e and M ≥L ≥1, every pair of roots a, b ∈BL(x) of F and G, respectively, satisfies
b −a ∈pMZd
p. The perturbed root is unique in that ball whenever it exists.
Proof. Since p | c, differentiation gives DG(x) ≡DF(x) (mod p). Truncate a to an integer
vector y at depth M. Then F(y) ≡0 (mod pM), hence also G(y) ≡0 (mod pM). Congruence
of y and x modulo p preserves the reduced Jacobian. The smooth root theorem gives b ≡y ≡a
(mod pM).
In the square case this constructed root lies in BL(x) because M ≥L. Square uniqueness
for G identifies it with every competing root in that ball. The construction therefore gives the
precision assertion for an arbitrary root there.
See Komorebi.PolynomialRootStability.exists_nearby_perturbed_root and Komorebi.PolynomialR
ootStability.perturbed_roots_congruent. The scalar c may be negative or zero. The weighted
theorems below allow arbitrary integral p-adic coefficients and give an explicit first-order
displacement.
Theorem 10.12 (Continuous charts with a selected Jacobian minor). Let p be prime, L ≥1,
F ∈Z[X1, . . . , Xe+k]e, and x ∈Ze+k satisfy F(x) ∈pLZe. Suppose the full reduced Jacobian

Proof. First consider a square integer polynomial map P whose reduced Jacobian at x is
surjective. Its Jacobian is invertible over Fp. A digit comparison using Taylor expansion shows
that equal outputs modulo pN+1 force equal inputs modulo pN+1 whenever the inputs already
agree modulo pN and belong to B1(x). Induction and integer truncation give, for arbitrary
a, b ∈B1(x),
P(a) ≡P(b)
(mod pN)
⇐⇒
a ≡b
(mod pN).
More generally, for a rectangular map with surjective reduced Jacobian,
P(BL(x)) = P(x) + pLZe
p.
(10.9)
One inclusion follows from polynomial congruence. For integer targets in the right-hand ball,
subtract the target from the constant terms and use the smooth root theorem. For arbitrary
p-adic targets, approximate by integer truncations. The input ball is compact, so its continuous
image is closed. It contains all these approximants and hence their limit. This argument does
not assume that independently chosen preimages form a compatible sequence.
For square P, the resulting bijection between the two compact residue balls is continuous,
and its target is Hausdorff. It is therefore a homeomorphism, with the same congruence
preservation for its inverse.
Now suppose a chosen set of e columns of DF(x) mod p is independent, and put those
coordinates first. Apply the square result to the augmented polynomial map
Ψ(Y, T) = (F(Y, T), T).
Its reduced Jacobian has upper blocks (DY F, DT F) and lower blocks (0, Ik), so it is invertible.
For t ∈T, the target (0, t) belongs to Ψ(x) + pLZe+k
p
because F(x) ≡0 (mod pL). The inverse
of Ψ at that target gives the unique root with parameters t. The inverse congruence property
gives one direction of (10.8); projection onto the parameter coordinates gives the other.
Finally, surjectivity of the original rectangular Jacobian guarantees a basis among its
columns.
Extend the corresponding injection of coordinate indices to a permutation σ.
Renaming the variables by σ−1 transports polynomial evaluation and every formal partial
derivative. The fixed-coordinate construction therefore applies to the original system after
undoing the permutation.
The main theorem is Komorebi.PolynomialGeneralChart.exists_parameter_homeomorph. The
square local inverse and surjectivity for arbitrary targets are Komorebi.PolynomialLocalInverse.
localHomeomorph and Komorebi.PolynomialLocalSurjective.exists_padic_preimage. The center x is
only an approximate root: the chart need not satisfy ϕ(t0) = y0 unless F(x) = 0 exactly. The
assertion selects suitable coordinates; it does not permit an arbitrary prescribed solved block.

Theorem 10.14 (Every compatible integer parameter path). In the preceding notation, assume

Proof. At level N = L+n, write the supplied parameter increment exactly as tn+1 −tn = qNw
with w ∈Zk. Surjectivity of the solved block gives a digit u satisfying Jznu = −F(zn)/qN −

while congruence modulo q preserves the full reduced Jacobian. Construct the sequence
recursively, preserving the exact parameter coordinates and the fixed solved Jacobian. The
earlier cross-level argument proves all the stated integer congruences.
This is Komorebi.PolynomialPrescribedIntegerTower.exists_prescribed_tower_strong. In
particular, no assumption about solvability at each later level has been added to the initial
surjectivity condition.

The solved Jacobian remains surjective, so every shifted obstruction [r + Kznv] vanishes.

uniqueness of the actual endpoint, not of the integer representatives used to approach it.

endpoint uniqueness is false in general: the system F(Y1, Y2, T) = Y1 has two distinct roots
(0, 0, 0) and (0, 2, 0) in the same dyadic ball with identical parameter value.
10.7
Weighted lifting for a nonunit Jacobian
The remaining theorems in this section allow arbitrary coefficients and centers in R = Zp.
They concern square solved Jacobians invertible over Qp, even when their reductions modulo
p are singular. The exponent controlling an integral inverse is retained explicitly.
Theorem 10.16 (Weighted nonlinear lifting). Let p be prime, d, s, r ∈N, F ∈R[X1, . . . , Xd]d,
and a ∈Rd. Set J = DF(a). Suppose
J ∈GLd(Qp),
psJ−1 ∈Md(R),
F(a) ∈prRd,
r > 2s.
(10.11)
Then there is exactly one root b of F in a + ps+1Rd, and it satisfies
b −a ∈pr−sRd.
(10.12)
Uniqueness holds throughout the full ball of depth s + 1.
Proof. Let A = psJ−1 ∈Md(R). Inclusion into Qp and its injectivity give the two integral
identities
AJ = JA = psId.
Komorebi P.
56


Euler congruences / Paper IX
Final strengthened edition
These identities are also an equivalent integral certificate for the first two hypotheses of (10.11).
In particular, A is injective: Aw = 0 implies psw = JAw = 0, hence w = 0.
Precondition the polynomial system by A and set c = ps+1. Its derivative at a is AJ = psId.
Lemma 10.1 gives a polynomial vector Q with Q(0) = 0 and
(AF)(a + cu) = (AF)(a) + p2s+1u + p2s+2Q(u).
Since A is integral, (AF)(a) ∈prRd.
The strict integer inequality r > 2s permits k =
r −(2s + 1) ≥0 and a vector C ∈pkRd such that (AF)(a) = p2s+1C. The normalized root
equation is therefore
u = −C −pQ(u).
(10.13)
Integral polynomial evaluation is 1-Lipschitz for the supremum p-adic metric: a common
divisibility of input differences is preserved under addition and multiplication by integral
elements. Consequently the right side of (10.13) has contraction constant |p|p < 1 on the
complete space Rd. It has a unique fixed point u.
To obtain the sharper displacement, begin with u ∈Rd. If u ∈pnRd, then Q(0) = 0 and
polynomial congruence give Q(u) ∈pnRd. For n < k, both C and pQ(u) are divisible by
pn+1. Equation (10.13) therefore improves the divisibility of u by one digit. Induction gives
u ∈pkRd. Thus b = a + ps+1u satisfies (10.12), because s + 1 + k = r −s. Its normalized
preconditioned value vanishes, and injectivity of A gives F(b) = 0.
Every competing root z ∈a + ps+1Rd can be written z = a + ps+1w. Its root equation
implies the same fixed-point equation after cancellation of the nonzero scalar p2s+1. Contraction
uniqueness gives w = u, proving uniqueness on the entire stated ball.
The existence and uniqueness statements are Komorebi.PolynomialWeightedLift.exists_w
eighted_root and Komorebi.PolynomialWeightedLift.exists_unique_weighted_root. The inverse
hypothesis in Lean specifies an integral matrix whose image is exactly psJ−1; it does not
substitute a potentially larger determinant valuation for s.
Example 10.17 (Strict threshold and displacement boundary). For p = 2, F(X) = X2 + 3,
and a = 1, one has F(a) = 4 and DF(a) = 2. Thus s = 1 and r = 2 satisfy every displayed
hypothesis except the strict inequality. No root exists in Z2, since none exists modulo eight.
Hence r ≥2s cannot replace r > 2s uniformly. Also, F(X) = psX + pr has root −pr−s, whose
displacement is not divisible by pr−s+1. The guaranteed displacement cannot uniformly gain a
digit.

Example 10.20 (First-order sign and error precision). For p = 2, s = 0, M = 2, F(Y, T) =
Y + T, H = 1, a = 0, and v = 1, the prescribed parameter is τ = 4 and the solved root is
y = −8. Here J = K = 1, and the forcing term is H(a) + Kv = 2. Omitting the parameter
term would predict −4, and reversing its sign would predict 0. The corresponding errors −4
and −8 are both outside 22MZ2 = 16Z2. Thus either change would violate the stated error
bound.
The error exponent cannot uniformly be increased. For F(Y, T) = Y + Y 2 + T, H = 0,
a = 0, p = 2, s = 0, M = 1, and v = 1, there is an even root y of y + y2 + 2 = 0. Its equation
forces y ≡2 (mod 4) and y + 2 = −y2, so
4 | y + 2,
8 ∤y + 2.
Thus the error depth 2M −3s = 2 is attained even for parameter-only motion. The same
quadratic with a constant coefficient perturbation gives the coefficient-only boundary. These
examples refute a uniform extra error digit; they do not assert optimality for every individual
choice of p, s, M.
The combined results establish finite singular obstructions, smooth compatible towers,
full continuous root charts after a suitable coordinate choice, and quantitative deformation
for a rationally invertible solved Jacobian. Rationally rank-deficient persistence remains a
separate problem. The analytic solution-map theorem in the next section supplies the regularity
conclusion for these same derivative blocks.
11
Analytic regularity of the local solution map
The deformation estimates control how far an exact root moves under a small perturbation. We
now show that its local dependence on the parameters is analytic, and compute the derivative
Komorebi P.
59


Euler congruences / Paper IX
Final strengthened edition
from the polynomial Jacobian. The analytic argument takes place over a complete normed
field. For integral p-adic data, we first include the coefficients and base point in Qp; Zp itself
is not a vector space over Qp.
Let K be a complete nontrivially normed field. Let e, k ∈N, and write the variables as
(Y, T) ∈Ke × Kk. For
F ∈K[Y1, . . . , Ye, T1, . . . , Tk]e,
a = (y0, t0),
define the matrices of evaluated formal partial derivatives
J = DY F(a) ∈Me(K),
K = DT F(a) ∈Me×k(K).
(11.1)
Rows index equations and columns index variables. We first establish that these formal
derivatives are the derivatives of the evaluation map.
Lemma 11.1 (Derivative of polynomial evaluation). For P ∈K[X1, . . . , Xn], polynomial
evaluation is analytic at every point. At a ∈Kn its strict Fréchet derivative is
v 7−→

(∂jP)(a)vj.
For a finite system, its strict derivative is the continuous linear map represented by the matrix
of evaluated formal partial derivatives.
Proof. For the derivative statement, use polynomial induction on constants, addition, and
multiplication by a variable. Constants have zero derivative. The sum rule agrees with the
additivity of formal partial differentiation. For PXj, the product rule gives
d(PXj)a(v) = aj dPa(v) + P(a)vj,
which agrees with the evaluated formal product rule for every coordinate. The finite product
rule for maps to coordinate spaces then assembles the system derivative. Each matrix defines a
continuous linear map because the coordinate spaces are finite-dimensional over the complete
field. Polynomial analyticity follows from the corresponding polynomial induction in Mathlib
and its finite-coordinate assembly.
The derivative bridge and analytic statements are Komorebi.PolynomialAnalyticEvaluation.
hasStrictFDerivAt_evalSystem, Komorebi.PolynomialAnalyticEvaluation.analyticAt_evalSystem, and
Komorebi.PolynomialAnalyticEvaluation.analyticOnNhd_evalSystem.

Theorem 11.3 (Analytic local root graph). Suppose F(a) = 0 and J ∈GLe(K). There are
open neighborhoods t0 ∈U ⊆Kk and y0 ∈V ⊆Ke, and a map ψ : Kk →Ke, such that

(11.5)
In particular, ψ is differentiable on U.
Proof. Let g be the analytic local inverse of Φ supplied by Lemma 11.2. Since F(a) = 0,
Φ(a) = (0, t0) and g(0, t0) = a. Define
ψ(t) = πY g(0, t),
where πY projects to the solved coordinates. This gives the base value and analyticity at t0.

To obtain the root equivalence on a product neighborhood, we choose the neighborhoods
as follows. The source of the open partial homeomorphism defined by Φ is open around a; its
inverse is g. Choose open sets V and U0 containing y0 and t0 such that V × U0 lies in that
source. Restrict U0 further by the preimage of an open analytic inverse neighborhood under
t 7→(0, t), the open locus where this same ψ is analytic, and the interior of ψ−1(V ). All of
these sets contain t0. For the last set, use the continuity of ψ at t0 and ψ(t0) = y0 ∈V . Their
intersection is an open neighborhood U with ψ(U) ⊆V .
If t ∈U, y ∈V and F(y, t) = 0, then Φ(y, t) = (0, t). The left inverse identity forces
g(0, t) = (y, t), hence y = ψ(t). Conversely, the right inverse identity Φ(g(0, t)) = (0, t) says
both that its solved equations vanish and that its parameter coordinates are exactly t. Thus
g(0, t) = (ψ(t), t) and F(ψ(t), t) = 0. Analyticity at every point of U implies differentiability
there.
This is Komorebi.PolynomialAnalyticImplicit.exists_analytic_implicit. Differentiability is
also included explicitly in the statement contract Komorebi.PolynomialRegularityContracts.actua
l_padic_analytic_root_graph.
Theorem 11.4 (Regularity of a supplied root selection). Under the hypotheses of Theorem 11.3,
let h : Kk →Ke be continuous at t0, with h(t0) = y0. Suppose F(h(t), t) = 0 for every t in
some neighborhood of t0. Then h and ψ agree in a neighborhood of t0. Consequently h is
analytic there as a germ and has strict derivative Dh(t0) = −J−1K.
Proof. Continuity at t0 places h(t) inside V for all sufficiently close parameters. Intersect this
neighborhood with U and the neighborhood on which the root equation holds. Equation (11.4)
gives h(t) = ψ(t) throughout the intersection. Analyticity and strict differentiability at a point
are unchanged by agreement on a neighborhood.

Corollary 11.6 (Integral coefficients and centers). Let p be any prime. Suppose F ∈Zp[Y, T]e,
a ∈Ze+k

invertible over the fraction field.
Example 11.7 (The derivative sign and a nonlinear control). For F(Y, T) = Y + T, the exact
branch is ψ(T) = −T and J = K = 1. The derivative is −1. Uniqueness of the derivative
rules out +1 over Qp, including p = 2. The nonlinear polynomial F(Y, T) = Y + Y 2 + T
has F(0, 0) = 0 and J = K = 1 there. Applying the formal theorem to this polynomial
constructs an analytic local root graph with derivative −1. Thus the example checks the
existence conclusion as well as the derivative formula. The declarations and the deliberately
rejected positive-sign statement are in the regularity control and statement-contract files.
The results cover arbitrary finite e and k, including zero. The local inverse machinery
explicitly handles a zero-dimensional domain. The analytic theorem does not assign a uniform
numerical radius to its neighborhoods. The earlier weighted lifting and joint deformation
theorems retain their quantitative radii and error bounds, while the present statements establish
regularity of the local solution germ. A direct Lean identification with each historical subtype-
valued chart is an additional interface task. General persistence at a fraction-field singular
solved block is a separate problem.

12
Open questions and further directions
The arithmetic results above resolve Nishibuchi’s conjecture throughout its stated parameter
range. The local analyticity and differentiability theorem is also complete under its stated
invertibility hypothesis.
What remains is a set of extensions and sharper classifications,
together with the broader questions raised by the original native seed-calculus program.
12.1
Singular persistence beyond an invertible solved block
The weighted lifting theorem allows the solved Jacobian to be noninvertible modulo p provided
that it is invertible over Qp with a controlled inverse. This includes a substantial class of lifts
often called singular modulo p. The next problem is to understand Jacobians that remain
rank-deficient over Qp, where the inverse construction is no longer available.
For a fixed polynomial system F ∈Z[X1, . . . , Xd]e, a precision L ≥1, and a root ¯x modulo
pL, let
LF,L(¯x) = {x′ ∈(Z/pL+1Z)d : F(x′) = 0, x′ ≡¯x
(mod pL)}.
The finite obstruction theorem decides whether this set is empty from the residual digit and
reduced Jacobian. When it is nonempty, the theorem also determines its cardinality and affine
kernel structure. The conclusion concerns one level: vanishing of the obstruction permits the
next step, but does not guarantee that subsequent steps are possible.
For example, X2 −5 has the root 1 modulo 2, and both 1 and 3 lift it to roots modulo 4.
Every odd square is 1 modulo 8, so neither branch continues to a root modulo 8. Thus even a
successful first correction can lead to branches that stop at the following level.
We seek structural hypotheses and higher obstruction data that control an entire singular
branch, including its behavior under coefficient and parameter deformation. One route is to
propagate suitable conditions from level to level. Another is to prove existence at all levels
in a form that yields compatible branches. The finite obstruction calculus supplies the tests
for individual levels, but a persistence theorem needs a further argument linking them. Any
general formulation must allow multiple branches, later obstructions, and changes in the rank
of the Jacobian.
12.2
Native 13.2: presentation invariance
The original conjecture asks for a canonical finite obstruction packet whose vanishing is
necessary and sufficient for a one-level lift in a finitely presented arithmetic seed system. For a
fixed finite polynomial presentation, the paper now supplies the precise obstruction
oF,¯x = [r] ∈coker

JF,¯x : Fd
p −→Fe
p

,
where r is the residual digit. We have proved independence from the chosen integer represen-
tative. Invariance under a change of equations or presentation requires more.
The raw cokernel already reveals the difficulty. The presentations F = (X) and G = (X, 0)
describe the same zero locus over every coefficient ring. At zero their Jacobians are, respectively,
Fp
1−→Fp,
Fp
(1,0)
−−−→F2
p.
Their raw cokernels are 0 and Fp. Both distinguished obstruction classes vanish, but the
ambient obstruction groups are not isomorphic. The example rules out the raw cokernel as a
presentation-independent object while leaving open the possibility of an intrinsic obstruction
construction.
A precise form of Native 13.2 therefore needs four ingredients:
Komorebi P.
63


Euler congruences / Paper IX
Final strengthened edition
1. a category of seed objects, including how the operators, relations, projectors, weights,
and topology enter an object;
2. specified changes of presentation and a proof that they preserve the actual lift problem;
3. an obstruction object that respects dependencies among the relations, together with
comparison maps or a universal property;
4. a precise meaning of “finite” and “canonical”, and a proof that vanishing is equivalent to
the intended one-step continuation.
For commutative algebra presentations, the relation module offers a natural way to account
for dependencies among listed equations. To extend this approach to the full seed vocabulary,
we would need an encoding that preserves the lift problem, together with a proof of that
preservation. This is one of the links still needed between the concrete obstruction theorem
and Native 13.2.
12.3
Native 13.3: finite compatibility, completion, and descent
The original proposed form is
X ≃Xwt
2
×C
′Y
p>2
Xp.
It leaves X, the distinguished subsets defining the restricted product, the maps to C, the
ambient category, and the universal meaning of “smallest” unspecified. In particular, one must
decide whether X is a global object or a completed object.
The linear compatibility theorem provides one concrete part of this program. If two local
observations J : U →V and K : U →W are linear maps from the same correction module,
define
CJ,K = U/(ker J + ker K).
The attainable observations im J and im K have canonical labels in this quotient. Equality of
labels is equivalent to simultaneous realization by a single correction. The joint image is
exactly the fiber product over CJ,K, with the proved linear universal property. The
declarations are Komorebi.SharedCorrection.labels_eq_iff_common,
Komorebi.SharedCorrection.joint_range_eq_fiber, and Komorebi.SharedCorrection.common_universal.
To apply this result arithmetically, one must construct the common correction module and
the observation maps, then show that the resulting compatibility condition agrees with the
intended arithmetic one.
Why the global/completed distinction is necessary.
Consider constant sequences with
integer initial value. Their global parameter space is Z, while their local parameter spaces are
Zp. Under the canonical local maps, the image of the global objects is the diagonal
D = {(n)p : n ∈Z} ⊆
Y
p
Zp.
This diagonal is dense: a basic open set specifies finitely many congruences, and the Chinese
remainder theorem provides an integer satisfying them. It is proper: the tuple with 2-adic
coordinate zero and all odd-adic coordinates one is not the image of an integer. Indeed an
integer zero in Z2 must be zero, contradicting its 3-adic coordinate.
Consequently this canonical global diagonal cannot be an equality locus
{(a, b) : f(a) = g(b)} ⊆Z2 ×
Y
p>2
Zp
Komorebi P.
64


Euler congruences / Paper IX
Final strengthened edition
for continuous maps to a Hausdorff space C. Such an equality locus is closed, as the inverse
image of the closed diagonal in C × C, whereas D is proper and dense. This topological
argument, retained from the native progress note, concerns the stated topological interpretation.
It has not been formalized in Lean for this manuscript.
Finite CRT reconstruction leads to the familiar completed object bZ = Q
p Zp, but does
not identify which local tuples come from a single global integer. A complete form of Native
13.3 must therefore specify finite-level compatibility, transition maps and completion, and any
additional global descent condition. If recurrence, spectral, or Frobenius information is to
supply that condition, it must be expressed through defined maps or constraints on the local
data, with a precise account of how they enter C.
The accompanying native/Native_Conjectures_Progress.md records further results
in restricted settings: an intrinsic finite Ext1 obstruction for finitely presented commutative
integer algebras, finite CRT reconstruction for polynomial solution sets, and a characterization
of rectangular finite relations by equality of common labels. The obstruction result is a stated
specialization of a classical external theorem; the CRT and rectangularity results have prose
proofs in the note. These are preserved mathematical progress, with the restrictions and
source records given there, rather than Lean completions of the general Native 13.2 and 13.3
statements.
12.4
Arithmetic refinements
Write UN,j(n) = j!CN,j(n) and Uj = U4,j, and use vp(0) = ∞when discussing valuation
bounds. The following classification questions remain open in this program.
1. The remaining higher-difference classification. The new strengthening chapters
determine every repeated-step valuation in addresses j = 0, 1, give the exact criterion
for equality in the common mixed-step lower bound there, and determine address-two
valuations at all power-of-two orders. What remains is a complete exact formula in
address two at the other orders, a full classification in address three, and the amount of
additional cancellation in mixed-step cases where the leading term vanishes. Numerical
patterns through a finite range do not settle these questions.
2. Upper-half addresses at larger dyadic rank. The exact first-difference laws now
include N = 2, 4, 8 and every lower-half address for N = 2a ≥4. Larger upper-half
addresses need a finer classification. Explicit counterexamples show that extrapolating
one low-order Newton coefficient to a universal metric law is false. A least period is
measured in packet rows; its Euler-degree displacement is NP.
3. Exact affine Mahler-kernel valuations. For the kernel of Section 2.7, determine
exact values of
vp
 ΘM,s(r, k)
,
ΘM,s(r, k) = [zr](1 + z)s((1 + z)M −1)k,
beyond the explicit special families. The minimum valuation of the summands gives a
lower bound. Equality requires a separate argument excluding cancellation.
4. Classical tail root classification and simplicity. The later tail program gives an
exact factorization, finite preparation for each fixed depth, and several infinite regular-
wall and simplicity families, as summarized in Appendix A. A universal explicit root
classification and universal simplicity of the remaining analytic factor are still open. In
the exceptional address-one quadratic reduction, the residual task is a nonvanishing
discriminant condition. Empty tails remain exactly zero. This problem is distinct from
the solved Nishibuchi congruence conjecture.
Komorebi P.
65


Euler congruences / Paper IX
Final strengthened edition
These refinements build on the proved estimates and exact cases, including the sharp
uniform classical dyadic bound and the resolution of Nishibuchi’s conjecture.
12.5
Historical material and interface work
The original Paper IX, with corrected proof-status labels, survives as a 28-page PDF. Its
recovered formalization supports the framework, Euler coordinates, Mahler differences, unit
coordinates, recurrence packets, and CRT results retained in this manuscript. The exact
original TeX bytes, original figure-generation files, and complete historical experiment package
were not recovered with that PDF. The reconstructed Annals text is reproduced in Appendix D,
with its separate provenance and local qualifications. Their older figure and test counts remain
historical records; those computations have not been reproduced for this consolidation.
A reconstructed textual copy of the Annals manuscript was also recovered. It includes
finite-field mode and subgroup inversion, Fourier invariance, Carmichael and matrix transfer,
Amice pullbacks, depleted analytic Euler branches, and extensive polyphase formulas. The
sharp classical dyadic kernel and truncation claims from that text have since been connected
to checked Lean proofs. The finite polyphase formulas used for explanation here have short
explicit algebraic derivations. The remaining claims in that larger historical text have not all
been formalized. The source archive retains the text with its provenance, and the statement
map distinguishes these historical claims from the certified claims in the present manuscript.
Remaining recovery work includes the precise older odd-prime edge identities, their stated
ranges, and the binary digit-sum form of the checked weights. For each item, the work is to
recover the formula with its stated range, check the proof, and identify any corresponding
Lean declaration. Where the historical source is still missing, the record remains incomplete.
One further task is to connect the older chart constructions to the new regularity theorem.
That theorem applies to polynomials with arbitrary coefficients in the ambient field Qp and
identifies the analytic germ of every continuous ambient-field root selection under its hypotheses.
Several older rectangular charts were defined using integer coefficients, integer centers, or
subtype-valued maps. Comparing each such historical interface explicitly with the new ambient
formulation remains to be done. These comparisons would connect the existing constructions
to the proved analytic implicit-function theorem. General singular persistence remains the
separate problem described in Section 12.1.
13
Formal evidence and reproduction
The verification record supports the manuscript in several ways. The Lean source states the
quantified theorem and its hypotheses. A compiler report records acceptance of that source by
a specified Lean and Mathlib environment. An axiom audit identifies the axioms used by the
resulting named declarations. Statement contracts and mathematical controls check selected
interfaces and boundary cases. Finally, source correspondence reviews compare the formal
objects with the intended mathematical statement. Each check has a specific role: numerical
examples test particular cases, whereas a universal claim requires a proof, and correspondence
with an external conjecture requires a comparison of the statements.
This edition revises the exposition and checks the evidence index against the preserved
sources. The Lean sources are unchanged. Their compiler acceptance is supported by the
earlier successful reports, whose source and log hashes are checked by the accompanying index.
The most recent compilation covers local analytic regularity and its polynomial dependencies;
earlier reports cover the arithmetic and native obstruction developments.
The proofreading pass also restores several results omitted from the first consolidation

and records the comparison with the original paper. Its coverage matrix and semantic review
appear in consolidation/editorial/. The Bernoulli boundary is accompanied by a classical
derivation, without a claim of a dedicated Lean certificate. The reviews distinguish this
addition from restored results already present in the checked sources.

13.2
Claim-to-source correspondence
The source references attached to the mathematical sections identify the principal declara-
tions for each result.
Their complete names, exact source hashes, roles and verification
frontiers are collected in consolidation/audit/DECLARATIONS.csv.
The accompanying
consolidation/audit/EVIDENCE_INDEX.json records the corresponding inventories, reports
and retained logs. In particular, the following interfaces distinguish the principal completed
results from generic auxiliary lemmas:
• The external conjecture is assembled by NishibuchiCompletion.conjecture_3_9 in the
original completion baseline. Its coefficient identification, integrality, parameter split and
explicit threshold are part of that proof chain.
• Exact N = 4 packet periods are supplied by DyadicFourLeastPeriods.least_packet_period;
the isometric extension is supplied by DyadicFourIsometry.existsUnique_isometry. The
normalization of the original coefficients is an explicit part of these interfaces.
• The classical sharp edge tower is supplied by DyadicSharpTower.dyadic_supercongruence_sh
arp, together with its first omitted error valuation. Its optimality concerns the stated
uniform truncation bound.
• Prescribed parameter lifting uses the actual residual and actual partial Jacobians in
PolynomialPrescribedObstruction.prescribed_lift_iff_obstruction_zero. The quotient
criterion and exact lift counts are not assumptions of the later tower results.
• Joint deformation is supplied by PolynomialJointDeformation.exists_unique_joint_deforma
tion. Local regularity is supplied by PolynomialAnalyticImplicit.exists_analytic_implicit,
with the actual polynomial derivative and the derivative formula −J−1K.
• PolynomialAnalyticSelection.continuous_selection_regularity transfers the analytic germ
and strict derivative to any continuous local root selection satisfying the stated root and
base-point conditions.
Here arithmetic declaration names have namespace prefix KomorebiPaperIX.; polynomial and
native declarations have prefix Komorebi.. The section maps and CSV retain the full names.
The declaration index covers every explicitly named theorem, lemma, definition and
abbreviation in the 14 release-frontier inventories, together with the earlier standalone affine
report. Byte-identical copies of a source file are collapsed, while all their locations remain
recorded. If the same qualified name occurs in different source versions, the versions remain
separate and are marked.
Anonymous examples, compiler-generated declarations, failed
development attempts, and the contents of earlier nested historical snapshots are not counted
as named results in this index. Negative-control source files are checked for integrity but are
not recorded as proved mathematical assertions. Consequently, the number of CSV rows must
not be quoted as a global unique theorem count.
13.3
External statement and library dependencies
The original conjecture correspondence was reviewed against Nishibuchi’s arXiv version
2605.10677v2, dated 1 June 2026. The retained source record identifies the official version,
the full title, the PDF checksum and the extracted-text checksum. The detailed review
compares Definition 2.1, Notation 3.2 and Conjecture 3.9 with the actual reciprocal EGF
coefficients, the factor j!, the residue range, the degree displacement, and the threshold in the
formal capstone. Nishibuchi’s conjecture is the conclusion to be proved; it is not an axiom
imported into Lean.
Komorebi P.
68


Euler congruences / Paper IX
Final strengthened edition
The full external paper is not redistributed in this source release. Consolidation preserves
the earlier source correspondence audit and its version record. The earlier consolidation
recorded a blocked request to the official arXiv endpoints. For the preserved prior edition,
the official unversioned PDF was inspected; its internal version is v2, dated 1 June 2026.
Definition 2.1 and Remark 2.3 (printed page 3), Notation 3.2 (page 5), and Conjecture 3.9
(page 8) confirm the parameter, index, degree-displacement and normalization match. Some
direct versioned-endpoint requests were blocked in that earlier check. The later strengthening
source review reached the primary PDF and HTML through the official record and confirmed
the same v2 statement. These are dated source-access records, not mathematical hypotheses.
This source check is distinct from the inherited compiler acceptance. No broad literature or
priority claim is made.
The mathematical environment is Lean 4.34.0-rc2, with Mathlib revision aeb72f1e
9981b6d35b426b16dca7759be3e7ba06.
The allowed axiom dependencies are propext,
Classical.choice and Quot.sound. The reports do not describe a rebuild of Mathlib from
source. The local regularity audit separately records the applicable Mathlib analytic inverse
and strict derivative interfaces and their hypotheses. In particular, the field-valued analysis is
performed over a complete nontrivially normed field; its integral specialization embeds the
data into Qp. It does not use Zp as a vector space over Qp.
13.4
Reproduction and limits of the consolidation check
The archive contains the mathematical sources, verifier scripts, controls, contracts, prior logs
and exact inventories. The pinned Lean and Mathlib kit is supplied separately. From the
consolidated archive root, the latest polynomial gate can be reproduced with a new output
directory:
python3 replay_paper9_regularity.py \
--kit /absolute/path/to/pinned-kit \
--output /absolute/path/to/new-polynomial-run
The preserved arithmetic, native and shared-correction chain has a separate entry point:
python3 replay_shared_correction.py --all \
--kit /absolute/path/to/pinned-kit \
--output /absolute/path/to/new-arithmetic-run
These commands reproduce the recorded checks; they were not rerun during manuscript
consolidation or proofreading. The second command rebuilds the preserved baseline before
the arithmetic continuation. Each output path must be new. The reports retain the original
full commands, runtime fingerprints and output hashes. The executable-path compatibility
adapter used in the recorded environment restores runtime path discovery; it does not alter
Lean’s mathematical kernel or the source statements.

A
Exact classical Euler tails and the remaining simplicity
problem
This appendix records the related classical Euler tail program at its stationary-separation
checkpoint of 5 October 2026. Its purpose is to state the strongest proved reduction and its
exact remaining obligation. The universal simplicity problem below is a separate question
from Nishibuchi’s Conjecture 3.9, which the congruence results of this paper resolve. It is also
separate from the presentation and gluing questions called Native 13.2 and Native 13.3 in the
original Paper IX.
The tail results have mathematical proofs, supplemented in the indicated families by
complete coefficient certificates and proved bounds for every omitted coefficient. Bounded
sampled values support implementation checks; they do not supply the universal quantifiers.
The latest continuation did not run Lean. Its mathematical claims must therefore be read
with the formal-coverage distinctions in the Lean guide.

Proposition A.1 (Sharp uniform classical truncation bound). For every M, N ≥0,
v2
 TM(N)
 ≥4M + 3.
For each fixed M, equality holds at N = M + 1.
Proof. The source A(n) is a dyadic unit for every nonnegative integer n. Each nonempty
summand has m ≥M + 1, so (A.2) and integrality of the binomial coefficient give the asserted
bound. At N = M + 1, only the first omitted summand remains; its binomial coefficient and
A(0) both equal one.
Thus later row-dependent cancellation can improve a particular tail, but cannot increase
the uniform exponent required to hold at every row.

follows from (A.6).

precision, or prove that all its dyadic roots are simple.

v2Gr(x) =
