Proof. If δ < ti, the unit perturbation has smaller valuation than the skeleton term throughout
Bi, so the valuation is exactly δ. Otherwise, after the ball substitution and division by 2ti,
the coefficientwise reduction is y + c for a constant c ∈F2. A restricted integral series with
this reduction is a dyadic isometry: division of its difference by y −z gives an odd linear
coefficient plus even higher contributions. Binary lifting therefore gives its unique root and
exact distance law. Evaluating at x = i gives the stated displacement, since Gr(i) = 2δV (i)

cases are also resolved: regular labels j ≥2 have no roots there; j = 1 has one simple odd root
at equality.
For example, j = 5 has (t0, . . . , t4) = (6, 3, 4, 3, 6). The exact root counts at distances
1, 2, 3, 4, 5, ≥6 are 0, 0, 2, 3, 3, 5. The older sufficient stages 3, 5, 7 are not the sharp emergence
stages, which are 3, 4, 6. The first irregular label, j = 17, has V ≡1 + X (mod 2); the
regular-wall theorem does not apply. Complete coefficient certificates close distances 1 through
19, and the general separation theorem gives all seventeen simple roots for δ ≥20. Those two
kinds of evidence together resolve that family.

Detailed continuation derivations
The following mathematical release notes preserve the detailed arguments behind the continu-

hypotheses. Source-declaration and build inventories are supplied separately in the archive.

B.1
Paper IX: finite polynomial lifting and shared corrections
Author: Komorebi P. — 8 September 2026.
This block supplies the actual nonlinear Taylor argument previously missing from the
Lean formalization. The statements concern a fixed finite list of integer polynomials. They
hold for every positive integer modulus, including composite moduli and modulus one. No
Jacobian-surjectivity assumption is needed.
B.1.1
Statement and notation
Let
F = (F1, . . . , Fe) ∈Z[X1, . . . , Xd]e,
q ≥1,
L ≥1,
x ∈Zd,
F(x) ≡0
(mod qL).
Write Rq = Z/qZ, let ρq denote coordinatewise reduction, and put
rx = F(x)/qL ∈Ze,
Ax = DF(x) : Zd −→Ze,
Jx = ρq ◦Ax.
Here Ax is computed from the actual formal partial derivatives of the input polynomials. The
Lean map Jx has domain Zd and codomain Re
q, viewed as integer modules. It factors through
reduction to Rd
q; the cokernel below is presented directly using its integer-domain image.
Define the finite obstruction module and its distinguished class by
Ox = Re
q/ im Jx,
ox = [ρq(rx)] ∈Ox.
The checked criterion is
ox = 0 ⇐⇒∃y ∈Zd :
y ≡x
(mod qL),
F(y) ≡0
(mod qL+1).
The residual exists by the lower congruence and is unique because q > 0. The quotient is finite
since its ambient module Re
q is finite. The theorem permits zero variables or zero equations as
well.
B.1.2
The checked Taylor bridge
For every P ∈Z[X1, . . . , Xd], integer a, and x, t ∈Zd, Lean proves
a2 | P(x + at) −P(x) −a
d
X
i=1
(∂iP)(x)ti.
The proof uses polynomial induction: constants, addition, and multiplication by a variable.
The product rule for the actual partial derivatives controls the multiplication step. It does not
assume a Taylor identity or a liftability condition.
Komorebi P.
79


Euler congruences / Paper IX
Final strengthened edition
Taking a = qL, the inequality 2L ≥L + 1 gives
F(x + qLt) ≡qL(rx + Axt)
(mod qL+1).
Since qL ̸= 0 in the integers, cancellation in divisibility yields
F(x + qLt) ≡0
(mod qL+1) ⇐⇒ρq(rx) + Jxt = 0.
Every integer representative of the lower residue has the form x + qLt, and this correction
equation has a solution exactly when ox = 0.
The positive-depth hypothesis is necessary for the general nonlinear theorem. For F(X) =
X2 −1, q = 2, L = 0, x = 0, the Jacobian is zero and the residual class is nonzero, yet y = 1
is a solution modulo two in the same residue modulo one. Affine systems retain the previously
checked L ≥0 theorem.
B.1.3
Independence of the integer representative
Suppose x′ = x + qLs represents the same lower residue, with exact residual rx′. Polyno-
mial evaluation respects coordinatewise congruences, including when applied to each partial
derivative. Hence
Jx′ = Jx,
ρq(rx′) = ρq(rx) + Jxs.
The two cokernels therefore have the same ambient module and the same defining submodule,
and
[ρq(rx′)] = [ρq(rx)].
Lean states this using one explicit common reduced map, avoiding an implicit comparison
between differently indexed quotient types. This establishes independence of the integer
representative within the fixed polynomial presentation.
Changing the polynomial presentation is a separate question. Appending a zero equation
can change the raw cokernel by adding a copy of Rq, even though it does not change the
solutions.
B.1.4
Two polynomial blocks and one shared lift
Let G ∈Z[X1, . . . , Xd]f be a second fixed system with the same variables and the same lower
root x. Let Kx = ρq ◦DG(x), and define
Cx = Zd/(ker Jx + ker Kx).
This quotient is finite: it is a quotient of the finite image of Jx, or equivalently of the finite
image of Kx.
Suppose u, v ∈Zd are separately valid corrections:

(mod qL+1).

(mod qL+1),

(mod qL+1).
The labels are independent of the choices of valid u and v. An unconditional version asks for
separately valid corrections with equal labels; it does not assume that each block is liftable.
Taylor transport turns a common correction t into the simultaneous equations Jxt = Jxu
and Kxt = Kxv.
The previously checked common-quotient theorem gives precisely the
displayed equivalence. Separate solvability alone does not imply joint solvability; the controls
include F(X) = X, G(X) = X −2, starting at x = 0 modulo two and lifting to modulus four.

This block formalizes the correction-digit torsor and prime-field counts from the previous

These formulas permit singular Jacobians and zero variables or zero equations. Nonsingularity

B.2.2
Why these are actual residue-class counts
For every positive integer q, including composite moduli and modulus one, and every L ≥0,
the checked map
Φx,L : (Z/qZ)d −→{y mod qL+1 : y ≡x
(mod qL)},
t 7−→x + qLet
is a bijection. Here et is the canonical integer representative of the digit vector. The entire residue
fiber therefore has qd elements. Injectivity is proved by cancelling qL in the corresponding
integer divisibility statement; surjectivity comes from the lower-residue congruence.
The formal type ActualLifts is a subtype of these genuine ZMod (qˆ(L+1)) residue
vectors. Its polynomial predicate is evaluated on canonical integer representatives and tested
for divisibility by qL+1. The checked polynomial congruence theorem makes that predicate
independent of the integer representative. Thus using canonical representatives does not
discard or duplicate residue classes.
At positive depth, the preceding actual multivariable Taylor theorem gives
F(x + pLet) ≡pL(rx + DF(x)et)
(mod pL+1).
The new bridge proves that the evaluated integer Jacobian reduces to the actual Fp-linear map
Ax. Restricting Φx,L therefore gives an explicit equivalence
{t ∈Fd

The proof uses the actual formal partial derivatives of the original polynomial list.

B.2.3
The torsor
Let Tx = {t : bx + Axt = 0}. Its natural kernel action is
k · t = k + t,
k ∈ker Ax.
For every pair u, t ∈Tx, exactly one kernel vector sends u to t, namely t −u. Thus every
nonempty correction fiber is a torsor for ker Ax. Choosing one valid digit u gives
Tx ≃ker Ax,
t 7−→t −u.
Lean constructs an explicit AddTorsor structure for the digit fiber, proves the unique translation
property, and supplies a chosen-point equivalence from the actual residue lift set to the kernel.
A basepoint is required for this equivalence, but the translation action itself does not depend
on choosing one.
The torsor and residue bijection remain valid over every positive modulus at positive
polynomial depth. The displayed powers involving vector-space rank are restricted to prime
moduli.
B.2.4
Connecting the old obstruction to the new count
The earlier obstruction used the integer-domain map
Jx = ρp ◦DF(x) : Zd →Fe

The new count uses Ax : Fd
p →Fe
p. Reduction of integer vectors onto finite vectors is surjective,
so the two maps have the same image after restricting scalars. Lean constructs a class-preserving
integer-linear equivalence

p/ im Jx ≃Fe

Consequently the cardinality theorem counts the original obstruction group, preserving its
distinguished residual class. It is not merely a formula for a separately introduced comparison
object.
Rank-nullity gives dim ker Ax = d −ρx, and the quotient dimension is e −ρx. Cardinalities
of finite vector spaces complete both formulas.
B.3
Paper IX: compatible polynomial towers and p-adic roots
Author: Komorebi P. — 8 September 2026.
This block completes the nonsingular branch of the passage from one-step polynomial
lifting to an infinite compatible tower and an actual p-adic root. Surjectivity is required only
of the reduced Jacobian at the initial root. The proof derives its persistence, constructs the
tower, proves convergence and verifies the original polynomial equations at the limit.
B.3.1
Main theorem
Let p be prime, L ≥1, and
F ∈Z[X1, . . . , Xd]e,
x ∈Zd,
F(x) ≡0
(mod pL).
Assume the actual reduced Jacobian
Ax = DF(x) mod p : Fd
p −→Fe
p

Euler congruences / Paper IX
Final strengthened edition
is surjective. Then Lean proves that there is a point
a ∈Zd
p,
F(a) = 0,
a ≡x
(mod pL).
Here the coefficients of the original integer polynomials are cast into Zp, and their multivariable
evaluation is exactly zero. This is an existence theorem for a fixed polynomial presentation
with the stated Jacobian hypothesis.
The stronger checked statement constructs zn ∈Zd, n ≥0, with
z0 = x,
F(zn) ≡0
(mod pL+n),
zn+1 ≡zn
(mod pL+n),
DF(zn) mod p = Ax,

The last inclusion retains every prescribed precision. It is a divisibility lower bound, not an
assertion that the error has exactly that valuation. An approximation can already equal the
limiting root.
B.3.2
Why the single initial hypothesis suffices
The one-step Taylor theorem already supplies the exact correction equation
bn + Aznt = 0,
bn = F(zn)/pL+n mod p.
Surjectivity supplies a correcting digit for every residual bn. The chosen correction gives an
actual new integer point zn+1, using the proved nonlinear Taylor remainder.
Every coordinate change is divisible by pL+n, hence by p. Each formal partial derivative is
itself an integer polynomial, so its value is unchanged modulo p. Therefore
Azn+1 = Azn = Ax.
The proof assumes no separate surjectivity or solvability statement at later levels. A dependent
recursion chooses each next point from the proved one-step existence theorem. It produces
one compatible sequence, rather than unrelated witnesses at different precisions.
The integer tower construction works more generally for every positive modulus q, including
composite q and q = 1, with a surjective map over Z/qZ. The p-adic completion and the
rank-based branching formulas in this block use prime p.
B.3.3
Convergence and exact polynomial vanishing
Compatibility implies, for m ≥n,
pL+n | zm −zn.
After casting to Zp, this bounds each coordinate distance by p−(L+n), so the sequence is
Cauchy. Completeness supplies its limit. Closed norm bounds show that the limit retains the
congruence at every stage.
The generic convergence theorem is proved independently of the polynomial application.
It also proves
pL+n | vn for all n
=⇒
vn −→0 in Zp.
Komorebi P.
83


Euler congruences / Paper IX
Final strengthened edition
Apply this to each integer sequence Fi(zn). Continuity of multivariable polynomial evaluation
also gives Fi(zn) →Fi(a). Uniqueness of limits then yields Fi(a) = 0.
The cast-evaluation identity is explicitly checked, so the continuous function is the evaluation
of the original polynomials. Convergence and polynomial vanishing are conclusions, not
assumptions of the main existence theorem.
B.3.4
Exact number of choices at each level
Surjectivity implies
rankFp Ax = e,
e ≤d.
Combining this with the preceding exact residue-lift count gives
#Lzn,L+n = pd−e
for every n.
These are distinct solutions modulo pL+n+1 lying above the selected point modulo pL+n. The
original finite obstruction group at each smooth point has cardinality one.
The theorem constructs one path through these finite choices. When d > e, it does not
claim that all paths have the same limit. The generic uniqueness theorem concerns the limit
of a fixed sequence only.
B.3.5
Boundary controls
The positive control theorems and rejected invalid proofs cover three distinctions.
1. An initial lift does not guarantee an infinite tower without the Jacobian
condition. X2 −5 has the odd lower root modulo two and a lift modulo four, but no
root modulo eight. Lean proves that no integer tower can continue through all precisions
in this example.
2. Surjective rectangular Jacobians do not give uniqueness of all roots. The
system F(X, Y ) = X has a surjective Jacobian and distinct dyadic roots (0, 0) and (0, 2),
both in the same residue modulo two.
3. The retained initial precision cannot be increased without justification. For
F(X) = X −2, zero is a lower root modulo two with surjective derivative, but its exact
root two does not retain zero modulo four.
The controls are theorem checks of these explicit examples. They supplement the universal
proof audit and do not replace it.
For several polynomial blocks, the hypothesis applies to their combined system. Surjectivity
of each block separately does not imply surjectivity of the combined Jacobian or shared liftability.
The preceding shared-correction obstruction remains relevant in that situation.

B.4.2
1. Unique roots for square systems
If d = e, Ax is surjective, and F(x) ≡0 (mod pL), then
∃! a ∈BL(x) : F(a) = 0.

Proof implemented. Map every arbitrary p-adic root into the actual residue rings Z/pnZ,

residue maps separate points, so the roots are equal. Existence is the preceding checked tower

This extends uniqueness from the limit of a selected sequence to all exact roots in the

B.4.3

The coordinate choice is a conclusion. A suitable minor and a preexisting chart are not

Since x is an approximate root, it does not assert ϕ(t0) = y0 unless F(x) = 0 exactly. The

B.5.2
Supporting theorems
For a rectangular integer polynomial system with a surjective reduced Jacobian at x, and
L ≥1,
F(x + pLZd
p) = F(x) + pLZe
p.
This covers arbitrary p-adic output targets. No lower-root hypothesis is needed.
When d = e, the actual restricted polynomial map is a homeomorphism. Moreover, for
arbitrary a, b ∈x + pZd

F(a) ≡F(b)

B.5.3
Proof structure
1. The actual integer Taylor formula gives one additional input digit from one additional
output digit when the reduced square Jacobian is surjective. Its finite domain and
codomain have the same size, so it is injective. Induction gives every integer precision.
2. Canonical integer truncations of arbitrary p-adic vectors preserve evaluation modulo
each power of p. Applying the integer result to truncations proves the p-adic congruence
equivalence.
3. Subtracting an integer target from the constant terms leaves the actual Jacobian un-
changed. The previous root-lifting theorem supplies a preimage. The image of the
compact input ball is closed and contains the convergent truncations of every admissible
p-adic target, hence contains the target. No compatibility of arbitrarily chosen preimages
is assumed.
4. In the square case, the resulting continuous bijection from a compact ball to a Hausdorff
ball is a homeomorphism. Its forward map is actual polynomial evaluation, and its
inverse solves the output equation.
5. For a specified surjective solved-variable block, apply this inverse to the actual augmented
polynomial map
Ψ(y, t) = (F(y, t), t).
Its derivative has an identity free-coordinate block. The inverse at (0, t) gives the root.
The lower-root congruence places this target in the output ball. Injectivity proves
uniqueness and full graph coverage. Projection identifies the inverse chart and reflects
congruences.
6. The columns of a surjective finite-dimensional linear map contain a basis. Select those
columns and extend their injection to a coordinate permutation. Polynomial renaming
by σ−1 transports actual evaluations and partial derivatives. The fixed-coordinate chart
then gives the main theorem on the entire original root locus.
B.5.4
Scope
Coefficients are integers; roots and parameters are arbitrary p-adic points. This supplies con-
tinuous polynomial parameter charts. Analyticity, differentiability, arbitrary p-adic coefficient

interfaces, weighted lifting for nonunit Jacobians, and first-order root-motion estimates are
separate.
Full Jacobian surjectivity need not allow an arbitrary prescribed parameter choice. At
the dyadic origin, F(X, T) = X2 −T has a surjective full Jacobian but zero X-derivative.
Prescribing T = 2 gives no root even modulo 4. Suitable coordinates instead solve for T.

B.6.1
The theorem
Let p be prime, R = Zp, K = Qp, and d, s, r ∈N. Let F = (F1, . . . , Fd) ∈R[X1, . . . , Xd]d,
a ∈Rd, and let

 ∂jFi(a)

i,j.

Then
∃! b ∈a + ps+1Rd : F(b) = 0,
b −a ∈pr−sRd.
All vector divisibility assertions are coordinatewise. The uniqueness predicate is the entire ball
of depth s + 1: competitors need not be assumed to have depth r −s. The theorem permits
arbitrary integral p-adic coefficients and centers, every prime including two, and dimension
zero. It retains the supplied inverse-denominator exponent s; there is no replacement by a
determinant valuation. Integrality of psJ−1 is expressed in Lean by existence of an integral

B.6.2
Proof
Write A = psJ−1 ∈Md(R). Then
AJ = JA = psI.
Komorebi P.
88


Euler congruences / Paper IX
Final strengthened edition
The formal matrix bridge proves the equivalence with the stated fraction-field hypotheses,
including nonsingularity. In particular, multiplication by A is injective, since multiplication by
J sends Av = 0 to psv = 0.
Put G = AF, t = ps+1, and k = r −(2s+1). Taylor expansion of the actual preconditioned
polynomials gives one polynomial system Q such that
G(a + tu) = G(a) + p2s+1u + p2s+2Q(u),
Q(0) = 0.
This expansion is proved first over every commutative ring. Its remainder is a polynomial valid
for every u, not a pointwise quotient depending on u. Since G(a) ∈prRd, it can be written
p2s+1C with C ∈pkRd. Thus
G(a + tu) = p2s+1 C + u + pQ(u)
.
Integral polynomial evaluation is 1-Lipschitz in the supremum p-adic metric. Consequently
T(u) = −C −pQ(u) has contraction constant |p|p < 1 on the complete space Rd, and has
exactly one fixed point u.
For completeness, the sharp precision is additional to this fixed-point existence. Inductively,
if u ∈pnRd, then polynomial congruence and Q(0) = 0 imply Q(u) ∈pnRd. For n < k, the
equation u = −C −pQ(u) therefore gives u ∈pn+1Rd. Starting at n = 0, this proves u ∈pkRd.
Hence b = a + tu satisfies
b −a ∈ps+1+kRd = pr−sRd.
The normalization and injectivity of A give F(b) = 0.
Every other z ∈a + ps+1Rd has the form a + tw. If F(z) = 0, the same normalization,
with cancellation of the nonzero scalar p2s+1, gives w = T(w). Fixed-point uniqueness implies
w = u, hence z = b.
B.6.3
Boundary and sharpness
The strict inequality cannot in general be weakened to r ≥2s: for p = 2, F(X) = X2 + 3, and
a = 1, we have F(a) = 4, DF(a) = 2, and may take s = 1, r = 2. Yet no root exists in Z2,
since there is none modulo eight. The auxiliary Lean controls prove this obstruction and the
exact residual depth.
The displacement exponent cannot uniformly be increased by one. For F(X) = psX + pr
at a = 0, with r > 2s, its root is b = −pr−s, which is not divisible by pr−s+1. The controls
prove the linear root and the absence of that extra digit for all primes.

Then there is exactly one root b of G in a + ps+1Rd. It satisfies
b −a ∈pM−sRd,
and there is ε ∈p2M−3sRd such that, in Kd,
b −a = −pMJ−1H(a) + ε.
The inverse in this formula belongs to the original Jacobian DF(a). The theorem constructs
the perturbed root from these hypotheses and proves uniqueness throughout the full ball of
depth s + 1. It does not require an additional Jacobian hypothesis on G. Coefficients and
centers are arbitrary integral p-adic elements. Every prime and finite square dimension are
included, including p = 2 and dimension zero.
The error depth exceeds the displacement depth by
(2M −3s) −(M −s) = M −2s ≥1.
Thus the first-order term determines strictly more digits than the displacement bound alone.
These are guaranteed divisibility bounds; equality of valuations is not asserted for every system.
B.7.2
Root construction with the original inverse
Write A = psJ−1 ∈Md(R), so AJ = JA = psI. Let t = ps+1 and k = M −(2s + 1) ≥0.
The actual polynomial Taylor and difference identities give polynomial systems QF , QH, both

(AF)(a + tu) = p2s+1u + p2s+2QF (u),

where

All exponents are nonnegative under M > 2s. Actual integral polynomial evaluation is 1-

fixed point inherits the depth k of C, because Q(0) = 0. Thus b = a + tu has displacement
depth s + 1 + k = M −s.
The certificate JA = psI makes multiplication by A injective, so the normalized zero is an
actual root of G. Every root in a + tRd has the form a + tw and satisfies the same fixed-point
equation. This proves uniqueness on the entire stated ball using the original matrix certificate.
B.7.3

Put δ = b −a and L = M −s. The displacement form of Taylor’s theorem, proved over any

EF = (AF)(b) −(AF)(a) −psδ ∈p2LRd.
Polynomial congruence gives

Euler congruences / Paper IX

Since F(a) = 0 and G(b) = 0,
psδ + pMAH(a) = −EF −pMEH.
We have M + L ≥2L, so the right side lies in p2LRd. Cancelling the nonzero scalar ps proves

Define this integral vector to be ε. Under R ,→K, the exact identity pM−sA = pMJ−1 gives

inclusions explicitly.
B.7.4
Sharpness and the strict threshold
For p = 2, take F(X) = X + X2, H(X) = 1, a = 0, s = 0, M = 1. There is a unique root
b ∈2Z2 of X + X2 + 2. Its equation gives b + 2 = −b2. It also forces b ≡2 (mod 4). Therefore
v2(b + 2) = 2 = 2M −3s.
The accompanying Lean controls show that the error is divisible by four and is not divisible by
eight. This refutes a uniform replacement of 2M −3s by 2M −3s + 1, even for a one-variable
unit Jacobian.
The prior strict-threshold example also applies to perturbations: take F(X) = X2 −1,
H(X) = 1, a = 1, p = 2, s = 1, M = 2. Then F(a) = 0, DF(a) = 2, and F + 22H = X2 + 3
has no root modulo eight, hence none in Z2. The new explanatory identification uses the
previously checked no-root polynomial. The strict inequality M > 2s cannot uniformly be
replaced by M ≥2s.
B.8
Paper IX: prescribed-parameter polynomial lifting
Komorebi P. — 8 September 2026.
B.8.1
Setup and exact lift set
Let q > 0, L ≥1, and m, k, e ≥0. Write S = Z/qZ. Let

F(x) ≡0
(mod qL).

r = q−LF(x) ∈Ze,

z ≡x

(mod qL+1).
The formal predicate uses canonical integer representatives for evaluation and the lower congru-

establish the actual residue-class interpretation. Changing an integral representative of a
parameter digit by a multiple of q does not change its prescribed residue modulo qL+1.

B.8.2
The obstruction theorem
Define the finite solved-block cokernel and its parameter-dependent class by
Cx = Se/ im J,
ox(v) = [¯r + Kv] ∈Cx.
Then
Lv ̸= ∅⇐⇒ox(v) = 0.
More precisely, the checked digit-to-lift equivalence is
Lv ∼= {u ∈Sm : Ju = −¯r −Kv}.
It sends a solved digit u to the actual next residue represented by x+qL(bu, bv). The obstruction
depends on the solved block J, not on the range of the full map (J K).
Every nonempty Lv has a checked free and transitive action of ker J. The action is defined
without choosing a base lift. Choosing one valid solved digit then gives an explicit bijection
Lv ∼= ker J. In particular, for every positive modulus,
ox(v) = 0
=⇒
#Lv = # ker J.
These results permit composite q, the degenerate modulus q = 1, and zero dimensions. Neither
J nor the full reduced Jacobian is assumed surjective.
B.8.3
Exact prime-field counts
When q = p is prime, put ρ = rankFp J. Then
#Lv =
(
pm−ρ,
ox(v) = 0,
0,
ox(v) ̸= 0,
#Cx = pe−ρ.
The count is of distinct actual residue classes at level L + 1. The solved dimension m, rather
than the total number m + k of variables, appears in the exponent because the parameter digit
is fixed. These rank formulas are asserted only over prime fields.
The main wrapper assumes only the original lower polynomial congruence. It constructs
one residual r and supplies the criterion and count uniformly for every v, together with the
exact obstruction cardinality.
B.8.4
Proof and dependency chain
The actual derivative calculation gives
(DF(x) mod q)(u, v) = Ju + Kv.
Taylor expansion at depth L ≥1 makes the quadratic remainder divisible by qL+1. Therefore
the digit (u, v) is a polynomial lifting correction exactly when
¯r + Ju + Kv = 0.
The quotient class [¯r + Kv] vanishes exactly when this equation admits u. This uses additive
inverses in the image of J, so the sign agrees with the actual Taylor equation.
The earlier unrestricted correction-digit equivalence is then restricted to the predicate that
the parameter digit equals v. Taking free coordinates commutes with forming digit residues,
Komorebi P.
92


Euler congruences / Paper IX
Final strengthened edition
and digit residues are injective in the digit. Consequently the restricted equivalence enforces
exactly the actual parameter residue condition on Lv.
Translation by ker J acts freely and transitively on the solved-digit fiber. Transporting
this explicit action through the residue equivalence yields the torsor on Lv. Rank-nullity over
Fp then gives the two displayed cardinalities. No numerical enumeration is used to establish
the general theorem.
B.8.5
Why the solved block matters
Take F(Y, T) = T, q = 2, L = 1, and x = (0, 0). The full Jacobian (0 1) is surjective, but J = 0.
Prescribing v = 1 forces T ≡2 (mod 4), which cannot satisfy F = 0 (mod 4). Prescribing
v = 0 gives exactly two lifts, corresponding to Y ≡0, 2 (mod 4).
The Lean controls check this actual polynomial example. Thus full-Jacobian surjectivity
alone does not make every prescribed coordinate choice admissible. The automatic coordinate
selection in the earlier chart theorem is compatible with this obstruction.

B.9.2
Proof
At depth N ≥1, suppose F(a) = qNr and a desired next integer parameter target satisfies

q satisfying
Jau = −¯r −Ka ¯w.

polynomial Taylor identity gives
F(a′) ≡F(a) + qNDF(a)(eu, w) ≡0
(mod qN+1).
Its quadratic remainder is divisible by q2N, and 2N ≥N + 1. Also a′ ≡a (mod q), so
evaluation of every polynomial partial derivative gives the same reduced Jacobian. Induction
on the level, carrying the root, exact parameters, and fixed solved Jacobian as its state,
constructs the entire integer tower. The existence proof uses ordinary classical choice; it does
not supply an optimized computational lifting algorithm.

τ −t(n) ∈pL+nZk

The compatible vector-tower completion theorem constructs b, proves convergence, and
retains every stage precision. Continuous polynomial evaluation and the increasing root
precision give F(b) = 0. Each parameter coordinate has both the corresponding coordinate of
b and τ as its limit, so uniqueness of limits gives exact parameter equality.

p/ im Ja. The fixed solved Jacobian is surjective, so this class vanishes for every digit at every

When m = e, the new solved-Jacobian definition is definitionally the existing square block
used by the checked continuous parameter chart. Every root in the initial ball with the given
parameters is that chart point. Applying this to any two roots proves equality, including
endpoints obtained by different constructions.
B.9.3
Scope controls
The inherited checked example F(Y, T) = T, p = 2, x = 0, L = 1 has a surjective full Jacobian
but zero solved block. Prescribing parameter digit 1 forces T = 2 (mod 4), so no next root
exists. Full-Jacobian surjectivity cannot replace the hypothesis on the prescribed solved block.
The new checked example F(Y1, Y2, T) = Y1 has a surjective solved block (1, 0). Nevertheless
(0, 0, 0) and (0, 2, 0) are distinct exact roots in the same modulo-two ball with identical
parameters. Uniqueness therefore requires the square solved case. The corresponding negative
control deliberately attempts the stronger false conclusion and must be rejected by Lean.
Depth L ≥1 is essential for the Taylor step used here. The general singular case can have a
nonzero next obstruction after an earlier zero obstruction. Neither the finite one-step criterion
nor this smooth theorem establishes unrestricted singular persistence.

For p = 2, s = 0, F(Y, T) = Y + T, H = 1, a = 0, and v = 1, both original blocks satisfy

The sign controls use M = 2: the actual augmented root is (y, τ) = (−8, 4), and the
required error ideal is 16Z2. Omitting Kv predicts −4, with error −4 /∈16Z2. Reversing its
sign predicts 0, with error −8 /∈16Z2. Both failures are checked. At binary depth M = 1, the
reversed-sign difference would lie in the allowed error ideal, so that shallower example alone
would not distinguish the sign. This control-design limitation was identified and corrected
before the release gate.

This block establishes both analyticity and differentiability of the actual local polynomial

C
Restricted native progress and complete prose proofs
This appendix reproduces the mathematical parts of the 8 September 2026 native progress note.
The polynomial obstruction, finite reconstruction, rectangularity and topological arguments
are prose proofs. The intrinsic commutative-algebra obstruction is the stated classical Stacks
specialization, with its original source record and finiteness argument. None of these prose
arguments is promoted to a new Lean run or to a completion of unrestricted Native 13.2 or
13.3.
C.1
2. A complete polynomial obstruction theorem
Scope.
Fix a prime p, an integer L ≥1, finite natural numbers d, r, and polynomials
F1, . . . , Fr ∈Z[X1, . . . , Xd]. Let ¯x ∈(Z/pLZ)d satisfy every equation Fi(¯x) = 0. Choose any
integer representative x ∈Zd. Set
ei = Fi(x)/pL
(mod p),
Jij = ∂Fi
∂Xj
(x)
(mod p).
The divisions are exact integer divisions. Regard J as a linear map Fd
p →Fr
p. Define
OF,¯x = Fr
p/ im J,
oF,¯x = [e].
Theorem. For this fixed polynomial presentation, the group and distinguished class are
independent of the chosen integer representative. The class vanishes if and only if ¯x lifts to a
solution modulo pL+1. The obstruction group has pr−rank J elements. When lifts exist, their
set is a torsor for ker J and contains exactly pd−rank J elements.
Proof. For every integer correction vector t, expansion of each monomial gives
F(x + pLt) ≡F(x) + pLJ(x)t
(mod pL+1).
(1)
Indeed, each term involving at least two correction factors is divisible by p2L, and 2L ≥L+1.
Constant and linear correction terms give the two displayed summands. Adding the monomial
expansions proves (1) for every polynomial, with no denominator or analytic convergence issue.
Every element reducing to ¯x is uniquely expressible modulo pL+1 as x + pLt with t ∈Fd
p.
By (1), it solves the equations exactly when
e + Jt = 0
in Fr
p.
(2)
Equation (2) is soluble exactly when e ∈im J: the sign makes no difference because the
image is a subspace. This is equivalent to [e] = 0.
Another representative has the form x′ = x + pLs. Since L ≥1, its Jacobian is the same
modulo p. Dividing (1) by pL shows that its residual digit is e′ = e + Js, so it gives exactly
the same quotient class. If t0 solves (2), all its solutions are t0 + ker J, and no others. The
finite cardinalities follow from rank-nullity. This also covers no variables, no equations, zero
Jacobian and singular solutions. □
The proof is a universal mathematical argument. The accompanying Python checks are
independently bounded evidence about an implementation, not a substitute for that argument.
The standalone Lean specialization does not formalize multivariable polynomial expansion (1),
unless its own checked source explicitly says so.

Euler congruences / Paper IX
Final strengthened edition
C.1.1
Necessary boundaries and exact examples
• A failed singular lift: p = 3, L = 1, F(X) = X2 −3, x = 0. Here e = −1 ̸= 0 in F3
and J = 0. There are no lifts of zero modulo 3 to a root modulo 9.
• Representative independence is a quotient assertion: for F(X) = X2 −1, p = 3,
L = 1, representatives 1 and 4 have residual digits 0 and 2. Their common Jacobian is 2,
with full image, and both give zero obstruction. Their corrected representatives 1 and 10
are the same root modulo 9.
• A successful step need not give indefinite continuation: for F(X) = X2 −5,
p = 2, the root 1 modulo 2 has zero obstruction and lifts to 1, 3 modulo 4. Both have
nonzero obstruction at the next step. There is no root modulo 8 and no compatible
infinite lift.
• The raw Jacobian cokernel need not be presentation independent: the presen-
tations F = (X) and G = (X, 0) define the same solution system. Their raw obstruction
groups at zero are respectively zero and Fp. Both actual distinguished obstructions are
zero. Thus one cannot claim a canonical isomorphism of these two raw groups merely
because the equations define the same seed.
C.2
3. An intrinsic finite obstruction under an algebraic restriction
Let B be a finitely presented commutative Z-algebra, p prime and L ≥1. For a ring map
f : B →Z/pLZ, use its reduction to make Fp a B-module. There is a canonical class
o(f) ∈Ext1
B(NLB/Z, Fp)
whose vanishing is equivalent to a lift B →Z/pL+1Z. This follows from the ring-map
lifting theorem in Stacks Project, Lemma 91.2.1, tag 08S5. Specialize the source extension to
0 →0 →B →B and the target extension to the kernel pL/pL+1 ∼= Fp. The base maps are
identities of Z. The kernel is square zero because L ≥1.
The comparison in Stacks Project, Lemma 10.134.2, tag 00S1 identifies the complex from
a polynomial presentation with the canonical naive cotangent complex up to homotopy. Thus
this construction is intrinsic, up to the stated canonical comparison.
The group is finite. Choose a finite presentation P = Z[X1, . . . , Xd] ↠B with finitely
generated kernel I. The presentation description in Stacks Project, Remark 91.2.4, tag 0GPU
identifies the group as
coker

HomB(ΩP/Z ⊗P B, Fp) −→HomB(I/I2, Fp)

.
If I has r chosen generators, evaluation on their classes injects the latter Hom module
into Fr
p. Hence its quotient is finite. The relation module I/I2 retains dependencies between
equations, including redundant zero equations, which the raw target Fr
p does not.
Status: externally proved after the exact stated specialization, with the finiteness argument
above. This theorem is not independently formalized in the accompanying Lean file. Extending
it to all operator/recurrence/module systems admitted by manuscript Definition 1.1 requires
a specified encoding and a proof that its notion of lift is preserved. That is the missing
implication for applying it to native Conjecture 13.2 without restriction.

C.3
4. A topological obstruction to the proposed spine formula
Consider the recurrence an+1 = an with integer initial value. Its global parameter space is
X = Z. Its local parameter spaces are Xp = Zp, and every local realization has the same
prime-independent recurrence. Give the local factors their usual p-adic topologies and the
product its product topology. For these integral local factors, the restricted product relative
to all Zp is the ordinary product. Write
A = Z2,
B =
Y
p>2
Zp,
D = {(n, (n)p>2) : n ∈Z} ⊂A × B.
Theorem. There are no Hausdorff space C and continuous maps f : A →C, g : B →C
for which the equality locus
{(a, b) : f(a) = g(b)}
is exactly D. Thus the canonical local realizations of global integer constant sequences can-
not satisfy the manuscript’s fiber-product formula in this Hausdorff topological interpretation.
Proof. The equality locus is closed: it is the inverse image of the diagonal of C × C under
the continuous map f × g, and a Hausdorff space has closed diagonal.
The set D is dense in A × B. A nonempty basic open set prescribes congruences at only
finitely many primes and finite precision at each. The moduli are pairwise coprime. The finite
Chinese remainder theorem supplies an integer meeting all those congruences, so every such
open set meets D.
However, D is not the whole product. The element with 2-adic coordinate zero and every
odd-adic coordinate one is not diagonal. If an integer had zero 2-adic coordinate, it would be
divisible by every power of two, forcing it to be zero. Its 3-adic coordinate would then be zero,
not one. Consequently D is a proper dense subset and is not closed. It cannot be the equality
locus above. □
This is not asserted to refute the original, underspecified sentence under every possible
reading. It refutes the explicit and natural interpretation just stated. The assumptions of
continuity, Hausdorffness and identification through the canonical local maps are essential to
this claim.
C.3.1
A precise reconstruction that does hold
For a finite set S of primes, positive precisions Lp, and a fixed integer polynomial system F,
put
N =
Y
p∈S
pLp,
SolF (R) = {x ∈Rd : F(x) = 0}.
Then coefficientwise CRT gives a canonical bijection
SolF (Z/NZ) ∼=
Y
p∈S
SolF (Z/pLpZ).
Komorebi P.
103


Euler congruences / Paper IX
Final strengthened edition
The reason is exact: the CRT ring isomorphism preserves polynomial evaluation, and
zero in a finite product is equivalent to zero in every component. For F empty, passing to
all compatible finite congruences identifies the profinite completion bZ with Q
p Zp. The same
finite reconstruction is compatible with coefficient projections and integer recurrence matrices
because these operations commute with reduction and CRT.
Here the polynomial equations or recurrence matrices are fixed common data. The theorem
reconstructs finite or profinite objects. It does not identify a “smallest” spine, impose additional
spectral descent, or recover global Z from arbitrary tuples of local points. A completed version
of 13.3 must say exactly which object is completed and specify its universal property.
C.3.2
A finite compatibility criterion with a precise universal property
Fix finite sets A, B of local packets and an explicitly given relation R ⊆A × B. Say that R is
rectangular when
R(a, b), R(a′, b), R(a′, b′)
=⇒
R(a, b′)
for all a, a′ ∈A and b, b′ ∈B.
Theorem. The relation R can be represented exactly as
R(a, b)
⇐⇒
f(a) = g(b)
for maps from A and B to a common set if and only if R is rectangular. In that case
a canonical finite choice of common set is the connected-component set CR of the bipartite
graph with vertex set A ⊔B and edges given by R.
The component maps fR : A →CR and gR : B →CR have the following universal property.
For every set Y and maps h : A →Y , k : B →Y satisfying h(a) = k(b) whenever R(a, b),
there is a unique map u : CR →Y with u ◦fR = h and u ◦gR = k.
Proof. Any equality representation is rectangular, since the three assumed equalities
imply f(a) = g(b) = f(a′) = g(b′).
Conversely, suppose R is rectangular. An edge always gives equal component labels. If
a ∈A and b ∈B have equal component labels, there is a finite path between them. Its length
is odd. A path of length one is already an R-edge. A longer path begins with three edges
a−b1 −a1 −b2. Rectangularity gives the edge a−b2. Replacing these three edges by that edge
shortens the path by two. Induction therefore yields R(a, b). This proves the exact equality
representation. Finiteness follows because the component set is a quotient of the finite set
A ⊔B.
For the universal property, combine h and k into a function on A ⊔B. It is constant across
every edge by hypothesis, hence along every finite path. It therefore descends to a well-defined
function on components. The two required factorization identities hold by construction. They
determine the value on every component, proving uniqueness. □
For example, the three pairs (a0, b0), (a1, b0), (a1, b1) without (a0, b1) fail rectangularity.
Every equality-label representation agreeing on those three pairs also admits the fourth pair,
so it cannot represent that relation exactly.
This is a precise finite gluing obligation: future arithmetic applications must define A, B
and R and prove rectangularity. The universal property holds for the component quotient
even when rectangularity fails, but then the equality locus strictly enlarges R. The theorem
establishes neither a minimum number of labels nor a smallest spine in the manuscript’s
Komorebi P.
104


Euler congruences / Paper IX
Final strengthened edition
unspecified category. It carries no automatic topology, spectral information or Frobenius
compatibility. Evidence: prose proof in this note, not a Lean formalization.
Komorebi P.

Euler congruences / Paper IX
Final strengthened edition
D
Recovered historical local polyphase manuscript
This is the complete substantive body of the reconstructed Annals textual copy, preserved
to retain its derivations and original parameter ranges. The recovered text is not certified as
the exact original TeX bytes. Its historical theorem headings distinguish these statements
from the current numbered core. Preservation is not a blanket proof audit or Lean certificate:
in particular the broader Amice analytic pullback and depleted branches, finite-field modal
minimality and unrestricted infinite polyphase claims retain their historical evidence scope.
The current formal statement map records only the individually identified interfaces in the
main text.
All 14 original figure inclusions refer to files absent from the recovered source; their captions
and labels are retained with an unavailable-source notice. The historical bibliography file is also
absent. Its citation keys are preserved in the bibliography as unresolved source records, without
invented bibliographic entries. Superseded claims about the divisor-branch finite-difference
problem have local notices directing the reader to the current theorem. The remaining higher
kernel-valuation and unrestricted local-to-global questions remain open in their stated scopes.
D.1
Introduction
Euler numbers carry several kinds of local information at once. Their indices have residue
addresses. Their coefficient transforms have triangular depth. Their local expansions have
Mahler degree. Their congruences depend on the valuation of the transfer kernel and on the
valuation of the source value in the row being reconstructed. These pieces should not be
merged into one undifferentiated congruence calculation.
This article begins with the Hurwitz product
(a ⋆b)n =
n
X
r=0
 
n
r

arbn−r.
Over a field of characteristic zero it is multiplication of exponential generating functions. Over
a finite field it realizes the additive group algebra through the exponential modes ϵu = (un)n≥0.
This finite algebra gives an exact proof of the standard window period. Hurwitz series rings are
treated in [15, 7]. The finite difference and umbral background may be found in [21, 10, 13].
Fix an odd prime p, a divisor m | p −1, and put
h = p −1

We prove that

The result first follows from subgroup symmetry in the finite Hurwitz algebra. A second proof
shows that the standard window operator preserves the finite space of sequences that are h
periodic after their constant term. The two arguments expose different parts of the same finite
character structure.
The central change in this edition is the treatment of the odd and dyadic Euler formulas.
They are no longer presented as unrelated reconstruction devices. For each prime p we define
Komorebi P.

Euler congruences / Paper IX
Final strengthened edition
an even source series Hp and an inverse kernel Kp satisfying
Kp(t)Hp(t) = sech t.
At an odd prime,
Hp(t) = cosh2(pt) sech t,

At the dyadic prime,

The coefficient convolution is identical in form, but the kernels have different integral weights.
This is the correct sense in which the local theories unify.
The dyadic kernel is substantially sharper than the separate coefficient bound alone suggests.
If

r≥0

Consequently, truncation after depth M is uniformly valid modulo
24M+3,
and this exponent is optimal. The rowwise estimate records the additional binary carries in the
binomial coefficient. Classical congruence theory for Euler families is extensive [8, 26, 27, 24, 9].
The formulas here are stated as exact coordinate identities. Historical priority requires a
separate theorem by theorem comparison.
The second expansion concerns local interpolation. The exponent of the finite unit group
moves every unit mode into a principal unit neighborhood. Binomial expansion then gives
a Mahler series whose depth r coefficient gains at least Lr digits when the chosen jump is
the identity modulo pL. The same argument applies to polynomial exponential modes and to
invertible matrix state systems. Mahler interpolation and the Amice transform are classical
[19, 6, 20]. The local unit structure and valuation tools used here are standard [16, 22, 25, 12].
The odd Euler source contains one nonunit base. Removing that base gives an analytic
unit source and an interpolation of a depleted Euler sequence on every even character branch.
The dyadic source is already analytic on Zp after a removable singularity is filled. It yields a
direct analytic interpolation of E2n on Z2. These branches are then refined by the substitution
x = Mq + s.
The induced Mahler coefficient transform is finite and explicit. Its kernels compose exactly. At
prime power radix they become sparse modulo the residue prime. Every monotone valuation
ladder survives the refinement.
The remaining sections treat arithmetic lift obstructions, recurrence packets, and Chinese
remainder reconstruction. The lifting obstruction is the residual defect modulo the image
of the declared correction map. A recurrence packet is governed by a jump matrix CN. If
one integral recurrence is fixed, all of its local realizations have one common source. These
statements do not prove that arbitrary unrelated local objects have a global origin.

Figure 1: The arithmetic coordinate chain. Residue address, local character, Mahler depth,
transfer weight, and reconstruction are recorded separately.
Four boundaries remain explicit. A period modulo p does not by itself lift to all powers
of p. The full finite difference estimate for every congruential reciprocal packet still requires
control of the triangular inverse. Exact higher valuations of the affine polyphase kernel are not
yet known in general. Chinese remainder reconstruction and finite adelic synchronization do
not prove a global source for arbitrary local systems.

Historical remark D.32. The theorem gives a period modulo p. A period modulo higher powers
requires additional control of the triangular inverse and cannot be inferred from the finite field
argument alone.

D.5
One local Euler source and kernel calculus
The odd and dyadic reconstructions have one convolutional form. Their sources, kernels, and
valuation profiles are different. We first construct each chart and then state the common
theorem.

D.6
Mahler transfer and local analytic branches
The finite character coordinate resolves the torsion part of a local unit. A sufficiently long
arithmetic jump moves the remaining unit into a principal unit neighborhood. The coarse
variable then has a convergent Mahler expansion with an explicit valuation slope.
D.6.1

convergent Mahler expansion [19, 20]. We use that theorem as a standard input when the
local variable is allowed to range over all of Zp.

D.7
Polyphase refinement of local Mahler coordinates
Polyphase refinement replaces one local branch by finitely many digit branches while preserving
exact synthesis. For sequences it is residue analysis. For local functions it is the digit tree of
Zp. In Mahler coordinates it becomes a finite triangular kernel.

original coefficients.

D.7.7
Character and polyphase coordinates
For odd p, write
n = j + (p −1)(s + paq),
with 0 ≤j < p −1 and 0 ≤s < pa. The address j records the finite character. The address s
records the principal unit digit. The variable q carries the remaining Mahler expansion.
At the dyadic prime, write
n = j + 2(s + 2aq),
with j ∈{0, 1} and 0 ≤s < 2a. The sign coordinate replaces the odd prime character
coordinate.
Historical proposition D.81 (Local phase lattice). For a unit mode un, the odd prime coordinate
gives
un = uj+(p−1)s 
u(p−1)paq .
The dyadic coordinate gives
un = uj+2s 
u2a+1q .
Thus finite character phase, local digit address, and Mahler depth are separate coordinates.
Proof. Substitute the two displayed decompositions of n.
Historical corollary D.82 (Polyphase refinement of the Euler branches). The analytic branches
in historical theorems D.62 and D.65 admit exact refinements under x = paq + s. Their refined
Mahler coefficients are obtained from Θpa,s. The original monotone valuation ladders are
preserved, and unit mode or invertible matrix representations gain one certified digit per
refinement level.
Proof. Apply historical theorems D.73, D.75 and D.79 and historical corollary D.53.
Historical remark D.83. Exact refinement does not imply that every branch is simpler. A
simplification theorem requires an additional invariant, such as Frobenius sparsity, valuation
gain, spectral collision, or a vanishing transfer block.

D.8
Arithmetic lift obstructions
A congruence class may solve an equation to one precision and fail at the next. The correct
obstruction is not an arbitrary residual vector. It is the residual vector modulo the image of
the declared correction map.
D.8.1
Linear lifting
Let p be a prime. Let
M ∈Mr,d(Zp),
b ∈Zr
p.
Suppose that xL ∈Zd
p satisfies
MxL ≡b
(mod pL)
for some L ≥1. Define the reduced residual
ρL(xL) = MxL −b
pL
(mod p) ∈Fr
p.
Write M for the reduction of M modulo p.
Historical definition D.84 (Linear lift obstruction). The linear obstruction at depth L is
Oblin
L (xL) = [ρL(xL)] ∈coker(M).
Historical theorem D.85 (One level linear lifting). A class represented by xL lifts to a solution
modulo pL+1 if and only if
Oblin
L (xL) = 0.
More precisely, a correction y ∈Fd
p gives a lift
xL+1 = xL + pLy
(mod pL+1)
if and only if
My = −ρL(xL).
When one correction exists, the set of all correction classes is a coset of ker M.
Proof. Modulo pL+1,
M(xL + pLy) −b = MxL −b + pLMy.
After division by pL and reduction modulo p, the lift condition becomes
ρL(xL) + My = 0.
This equation is solvable exactly when the residual lies in the image of M. If y0 is one solution,
every solution is y0 + z with z ∈ker M.
Historical proposition D.86 (Representative independence). The class Oblin
L (xL) depends only
on the solution class of xL modulo pL.
Proof. Replace xL by xL + pLz. The reduced residual changes by Mz. Its class in the cokernel
is unchanged.

Historical theorem D.90 (One level polynomial lifting). Let L ≥1. A class represented by xL
lifts to a zero of F modulo pL+1 if and only if
ObF

A correction y ∈Fd

Proof. The polynomial Taylor expansion gives

(mod pL+1).
Every term of degree at least two in the increment contains p2L, which is divisible by pL+1.

Historical proposition D.91 (Polynomial representative independence). The class ObF
L(xL)

Proof. If xL is replaced by xL +pLz, Taylor expansion shows that the reduced residual changes

Historical corollary D.92 (Simple root lifting). Assume r = d and
det JF (xL) ̸≡0
(mod p).
Then the depth L solution has exactly one child modulo pL+1. Iterating gives a unique p adic
zero above the initial class.
Proof. The reduced Jacobian is invertible, so every residual has one correction. The resulting
compatible sequence is Cauchy in Zd
p, and continuity of F gives a zero in the limit. Uniqueness
holds at every depth.
Historical example D.93 (A singular polynomial obstruction). Let p = 3 and consider the class
x1 = 0 modulo three. For
F0(x) = x2,
the residual and the reduced derivative both vanish. Every correction y ∈F3 gives
F0(3y) ≡0
(mod 9),
so the class has three children. For
F1(x) = x2 + 3,
the reduced derivative is still zero, but the reduced residual is one. Its class in the cokernel is
nonzero, so no lift modulo nine exists.
This is the usual simple root mechanism behind Hensel lifting. The obstruction formulation
also covers singular Jacobians, where some residual classes lift and others do not [22, 12, 20].

D.8.3
Packetwise obstruction splitting
Historical theorem D.94 (Packet decomposition of a lift obstruction). Suppose
Fd
p =
M
a∈A
Ua,
Fr
p =
M
a∈A
Va,

p →Fr
p satisfies
D(Ua) ⊆Va
for every a. Write Da = D|Ua. Then
coker D ∼=
M
a∈A
coker Da.
A residual lifts if and only if every packet obstruction vanishes.
Proof. The image of D is the direct sum of the images of the maps Da. Taking the quotient
of the codomain packet decomposition gives the stated cokernel decomposition.
Historical remark D.95. The obstruction theorems solve one declared local lifting step. They do
not prove that a compatible branch exists across unrelated primes, that a global Diophantine
point exists, or that an arithmetic representation is automorphic.
D.9
Recurrence packets and a common integral spine
Linear recurrences provide a setting where residue subsequences, local completions, and a
common global source can all be described exactly. Background on recurrence sequences may
be found in [11, 23].

Historical theorem D.97 (Characteristic polynomial of the jump). Let
χC(y) = det(yI −C).
Then
χCN (x) = Resy
 χC(y), x −yN.
The identity holds over the coefficient ring after interpreting the resultant universally.
Proof. After passage to an algebraic closure, let α1, . . . , αd be the eigenvalues of C, counted
with algebraic multiplicity. The resultant is
d

i=1
(x −αN
i ),
which is the characteristic polynomial of CN. Both sides are polynomial expressions with
integer coefficients in the entries of C, so the identity descends to every commutative coefficient
ring.
D.9.2
Second order recurrences
Historical theorem D.98 (Residue subsequences of a second order recurrence). Suppose
xn+2 = Pxn+1 −Qxn
over a commutative ring. Define V0 = 2, V1 = P, and
Vn+2 = PVn+1 −QVn.
For every positive integer N and every address j, the subsequence
uk = xNk+j
satisfies
uk+2 = VNuk+1 −QNuk.
Proof. The companion matrix
C =
 
P
−Q
1
0

has trace P and determinant Q. The trace of CN is VN, while its determinant is QN. Cayley
Hamilton applied to CN gives
(CN)2 −VNCN + QNI = 0.
Apply this identity to the residue state packet and take its scalar coordinate.

Historical corollary D.99 (Fibonacci residue packets). Let F0 = 0, F1 = 1, and Fn+2 =
Fn+1 + Fn. Let LN denote the Lucas number. For every N ≥1 and every address j,
FN(k+2)+j = LNFN(k+1)+j −(−1)NFNk+j.
Proof. Apply historical theorem D.98 with P = 1 and Q = −1. Then VN = LN.
D.9.3
Finite and local periodicity
Historical theorem D.100 (Periodicity of an invertible recurrence modulo a prime power). Let
C ∈Md(Z),
p ∤det C.
For every L ≥1, every state orbit modulo pL is purely periodic. Its period divides the order
of C in
GLd(Z/pLZ).
If p | det C, every orbit is still eventually periodic.
Proof. When p ∤det C, multiplication by C is a permutation of the finite state set (Z/pLZ)d.
Every orbit of a permutation is periodic, and applying the group order relation for C gives
the divisibility statement. Without invertibility, repeated application of a map on a finite set
eventually repeats a state, after which the orbit is periodic.
Historical corollary D.101 (Residue packet periods). Under the invertibility hypothesis of
historical theorem D.100, every residue packet with jump N has period dividing the order of
CN modulo pL.
Proof. Use the jump matrix from historical theorem D.96.
D.9.4
The common integral spine
Historical theorem D.102 (Base change of an integral recurrence). Let
C ∈Md(Z),
v0 ∈Zd.
The integral orbit
vn = Cnv0
determines, by base change, compatible orbits in every ring R equipped with a homomorphism
Z →R. In particular, the reductions modulo every positive integer and the orbits in every Zd
p
share the same companion matrix, characteristic polynomial, and integer recurrence relations.
Proof. Every coordinate of Cnv0 is an integer polynomial in the entries of C and v0. Applying
a ring homomorphism commutes with matrix multiplication and preserves every polynomial
relation with integer coefficients.
Historical corollary D.103 (Prime independent recurrence spine). An integral companion
system gives a canonical common source for all of its local realizations. No choice of coefficient
prime is required to define the recurrence or its residue packets.
Proof. Apply historical theorem D.102 to the companion matrix and its initial state.
Historical remark D.104. This theorem supplies a prime independent spine for integral linear
recurrences. It does not prove that every unrelated family of local arithmetic packets arises
from one global object.

D.10
Chinese remainder packet refinement
The Chinese remainder theorem gives an exact factorization of residue addresses. We state
it at the level of sequence projectors so that it can be combined directly with the earlier
coordinate systems.
Historical definition D.105 (Residue projector). For a sequence a = (an)n≥0, a positive integer

PN,r satisfy
PN,rPN,s = δrsPN,r
and

Proof. Every nonnegative integer belongs to exactly one residue class modulo N.
Historical theorem D.107 (Coprime packet factorization). Let A and B be coprime positive
integers. Let rA be an address modulo A and rB an address modulo B. Let r be the unique
address modulo AB satisfying
r ≡rA
(mod A),
r ≡rB
(mod B).
Then
PAB,r = PA,rAPB,rB = PB,rBPA,rA.
Proof. The product retains exactly the indices satisfying both congruences. By the Chinese
remainder theorem, those indices form one residue class modulo AB [14].

Historical proposition D.110 (Dyadic and odd residue refinement). Let N = 2am with m odd.
Every residue packet modulo N factors uniquely as the product of one dyadic packet modulo

Historical remark D.111. The residue address factors exactly, but valuation weights do not
become identical across primes. The odd coordinate system of appendix D.5 and the dyadic
coordinate system of appendix D.5 remain genuinely different integral charts.
D.11
Verification and exact boundaries
The proofs in the article are symbolic. Exact computation tests formulas, finite ranges, and
the source implementation. It does not replace a uniform proof.
D.11.1

Let Cmp,j(n) be a congruential reciprocal coefficient and let

inverse is applied. They do not yet determine every valuation loss introduced by that inverse.
The full family bound therefore remains open.

D.11.3
The remaining global gluing problem
The finite adelic theorem synchronizes a finite set of primes when one integral state system is
already fixed. The recurrence theorem gives a common local source under the same assumption.
Chinese remainder projectors reconstruct finite residue addresses. None of these results proves
that an arbitrary collection of local analytic branches has a common global arithmetic origin.
D.11.4
Historical scope
The article uses classical work on Euler congruences, finite differences, local fields, Hurwitz series,
Mahler interpolation, Lucas congruences, and recurrence sequences [8, 26, 27, 24, 9, 18, 17, 11].
The exact formulas and local transfer statements should be compared individually with that
literature before any historical priority claim is made.
Komorebi P.
145


Euler congruences / Paper IX
Final strengthened edition
D.12
Conclusion
The local arithmetic theory now has one common architecture. The finite character coordinate
identifies the torsion phase. The source and kernel product reconstructs the Euler coefficient.
The kernel valuation records certified precision. Carmichael and Mahler coordinates describe
the remaining principal unit motion. Polyphase analysis resolves additional local digits, records
carries and shifts exactly, and transports Mahler coefficients through a finite affine kernel.
The odd and dyadic cases agree at the level of convolution and differ at the level of
integral geometry. At odd primes the source must be depleted before it becomes a unit mode
interpolation. At the dyadic prime the source extends directly to an analytic integral function.
The dyadic kernel has exact weight 4r −1, giving the optimal uniform modulus 24M+3 after
depth M.
Three tasks remain outside the proved region. The first is a complete finite difference
estimate after the congruential triangular inverse. The second is an exact higher valuation
theory for the affine Mahler kernel. The third is a global existence theorem for local systems
that do not already arise from one integral source.
Language model contribution
Current status. This dated statement is superseded for the stated divisor-branch family
by Theorem 3.7, with the j! normalization and factorial valuation loss explicitly retained. It
makes no claim for unrestricted families.
An OpenAI language model was used as an organizational and mathematical drafting aid.
Its contributions included suggestions for the finite field Hurwitz proof, the unified source and
kernel formulation in appendix D.5, the sharp dyadic truncation in historical theorem D.47,
the Carmichael and Mahler statements in appendix D.6, the affine kernel in appendix D.7,
and the obstruction and recurrence organization. The author assumes full responsibility for
independently verifying every statement, calculation, proof, and reference in any submitted
version. The model is not an author.

Scope and result
This release continues the 28-page status-corrected Paper IX manuscript. It completes formal

Ten of 24 named manuscript-proved items are now covered in their recorded

A useful scope strengthening
The algebraic proof needs oddness, not primality. The exact reconstruction and the integer
divisibility statement are therefore checked for every positive odd length p, including p = 1
and odd composites. The manuscript’s odd-prime statement follows as a specialization. This is
a sharpening of assumptions in this development, not a historical-priority claim. Even lengths
are not included; a checked row-zero counterexample shows why.
What is not completed
The weighted dyadic theory and the general corrected odd-prime finite-difference theorem
remain pending. No continuum convergence or analytic summability theorem is inferred from
the formal power-series equalities in this block. The historical numerical study is not being
relabelled as a fresh experiment.

Even projection is identified coefficientwise
For any formal series f, let EvenPart(f) = (f(t) + f(−t))/2. Its nth coefficient is proved to
be the coefficient of f when n is even and zero otherwise. Rescaling the inverse law proves
sech(−t) = sech(t). The exponential multiplication identity then gives

Connecting to the paper’s Zn convention
The paper defines Zn through sec t+tan t. The Lean construction uses Mathlib’s actual formal
sine and cosine series and defines
sec t = (cos t)−1,
tan t = (sin t) sec t,

It proves the sine and cosine derivative identities, sin2 t + cos2 t = 1, and

Sign substitution in the squared variable carries the hyperbolic cosine kernel to the actual

the even coefficient in the derivative of tangent yields
bm = (−1)mZ2m+1.
This formula is proved, not assumed as an input to reconstruction. The formalization follows
the generating-function definition of zigzag numbers; it does not additionally formalize their
interpretation as counts of alternating permutations.

The scalar anchors are derived from the same general coefficient recurrence:
(E0, E2, E4, E6, E8) = (1, −1, 5, −61, 1385),

The completed mathematical chain
The inverse coefficients are defined from a genuine unit power series and connected to z2/ sinh2 z.
The source is the paper’s explicit rational formula, not an abstract integral input. Its valuation
calculation proves the stronger statement that every even source coefficient is a 2-adic unit.
Formal-series multiplication and coefficient extraction then yield the Euler identity with the
correct falling factorial. The omitted-tail theorem gives divisibility in the actual completed
integer ring Z2.
The clean cumulative run compiled all 17 modules, matched all 272 expected transitive
axiom reports and rejected eight invalid-proof controls. All 49 infrastructure and release tests
passed. Exact-rational regression checks are recorded separately from the universal proofs.
Completion boundary
This is a formalization companion, not a revised primary manuscript. The unnumbered
Bernoulli closed form for the inverse coefficients is not universally formalized here. Formal-
series identities do not establish analytic convergence. The corrected global odd-prime finite-
difference theorem remains pending. Historical numerical evidence is not presented as a fresh
reproduction.
1. Integral coefficients and an exact unit source
Lemmas 5.1 and 5.2; manuscript page 14
Komorebi P.

Final strengthened edition
Define the rational subring
O2 = {q ∈Q : |q|2 ≤1}.
Its ring laws follow from the actual nonarchimedean norm. For every q ∈O2, the code
constructs its element in Mathlib’s completed ring Z2, and proves that the underlying value in
Q2 is the rational cast of q. No integrality claim is hidden in a coercion.
The unit kernel and its reciprocal
Use a squared formal variable u and define
km =
22m+1
(2m + 2)!,
K(u) =
X
m≥0
kmum,
βm = [um]K(u)−1.
The constant term is proved to be one. The factorial valuation bound gives
v2(km) = 2m + 1 −v2((2m + 2)!) ≥0.
Thus every km belongs to O2. A general coefficientwise subring theorem is proved by strong
induction using the actual unit-inverse coefficient formula. Applied to K, it establishes
βm ∈O2
for every m ≥0.
The reciprocal’s coefficients are therefore proved integral; the proof does not assume them
integral in order to obtain the final congruence. Their connection to the intended hyperbolic
function is proved on the next page.
The source has valuation exactly zero
At even degree 2r, define the literal rational source
R2r =
32r+2 −1
4(2r + 1)(2r + 2).
Both numerator and denominator are nonzero. The pinned Mathlib lifting-the-exponent
theorem gives
v2(32r+2 −1) = 2 + v2(2r + 2).
Because 2r + 1 is odd, the denominator has the same valuation. Consequently,
v2(R2r) = 0,
|R2r|2 = 1.
The code proves actual IsUnit status for the constructed Z2 element. This strengthens the
manuscript’s integrality conclusion without changing its source. The argument applies at
r = 0, where R0 = 1, as well as at every later row.

a separate dyadic construction, not substitution of p = 2 into the odd-length theorem.

Two explicit specification boundaries
A finite sample through N determines ∆sf(n) only when n + s ≤N. For arbitrary orders, the

of the sample. All three versions are separately checked.
The additive valuation is extended to ordp(0) = ∞. This avoids incorrectly applying
Mathlib’s finite-valued default at zero to an inequality that must also cover cancellation and
identically zero differences.

p | up−1 −1
inside Zp.
The conclusion is not supplied as an abstract certificate. It follows from the unit hypothesis

Pinned Mathlib supplies finite-field, unit-group, ideal-power, p-adic residue and Chinese-

The specification matters
Rational coefficients are not reduced through an imaginary map Q →Fp. The proof first
constructs their integral lifts in Zp and establishes equality in Qp. Root representatives are
actual units at the requested precision, not unchanged residues that need not satisfy the root
equation modulo pℓ.

2. Explicit finite prime-power decomposition
Proposition 6.2; odd-prime branch
Let p be prime, ℓ≥1, and
a ∈
 Z/pℓZ
×.
Define actual units in the same ring by
ωℓ(a) = apℓ−1,
⟨a⟩ℓ= a ωℓ(a)−1.
The Lean source writes ℓ= L + 1, so zero precision is excluded without truncated natural-
number subtraction.
The root equation and residue are derived
Euler’s unit-group exponent gives
ωℓ(a)p−1 = apℓ−1(p−1) = aφ(pℓ) = 1.
Reduction to the actual field Fp commutes with powers. Its Frobenius identity gives
ωℓ(a) = ¯apℓ−1 = ¯a.
Consequently,
a = ωℓ(a)⟨a⟩ℓ,
ωℓ(a)p−1 = 1,
⟨a⟩ℓ= 1.
The manuscript’s finite existence conclusion is obtained directly. The proof does not assume a
Hensel lift or an already supplied root representative.
Uniqueness requires a lifting argument
The source proves the actual kernel statement
¯x = 0
⇐⇒
p | x
in Z/pℓZ.
For x ≡y (mod p), the checked ideal-power lifting theorem gives
xpℓ−1 −ypℓ−1 ∈(p)ℓ= (0).
If wp−1 = 1, then wp = w and hence wpk = w for every k. Therefore two roots with the same
residue are equal. The decomposition is unique whenever its second factor reduces to one.
Multiplicativity of both factors is also proved.
For p = 3, ℓ= 2, a = 2, the checked values are
ω2(2) = 8,
⟨2⟩2 = 7,
82 = 1,
8 · 7 = 2
in Z/9Z.
The unlifted representative 2 is not a square root of one modulo nine.

Finite refinements and Proposition 6.2’s dyadic branch
For 1 ≤k ≤ℓ, let
ρℓ,k : Z/pℓZ −→Z/pkZ
be the actual reduction homomorphism. It induces a map on units. The explicit construction
satisfies
ρℓ,k(ωℓ(a)) = ωk(ρℓ,k(a)),
ρℓ,k(⟨a⟩ℓ) = ⟨ρℓ,k(a)⟩k.
The proof uses the exponent factorization pℓ−1 = pk−1pℓ−k and the already proved Frobenius
invariance of a lifted root. Principal factors also satisfy
⟨a⟩pℓ−1
ℓ
= 1.
At precision one the principal factor is exactly one. These are coherent finite certificates; no
infinite inverse limit or topological unit-group equivalence is constructed in this release.
Why the mod-four branch is separate
Now let ℓ≥2 and a ∈(Z/2ℓZ)×. The actual units of Z/4Z are exhausted by 1 and −1. Define

⟨a⟩2,ℓ≡1
(mod 4).
The sign is uniquely determined by the residue modulo four, and cancellation gives uniqueness
of the principal factor.
The checked example a = 3 modulo eight has sign 7 = −1 and principal factor 5:
7 · 5 = 3
(mod 8),
5 = 1
(mod 4).

identify the sign. The generic prime-power construction at p = 2 only asks for a principal
residue modulo two and is not silently substituted for this stronger mod-four factorization.

CRT factors addresses, not the visibility weights or valuation filtrations associated with those

the standard window that is not 3-adically integral.

The principal statements are universal over their explicit inputs, not finite
numerical observations.
The main mathematical connection
A sampled state uk = vNk+j evolves by the actual jump matrix CN.
Its recurrence is
derived from the characteristic polynomial of that matrix. No scalar recurrence or spectral
decomposition is supplied as a substitute for the desired conclusion.
For Fibonacci numbers, the state is independently defined from Mathlib’s actual sequence.
The Lucas sequence is independently defined by its initial values and recurrence. The trace
and determinant of CN are then proved to supply the sampled recurrence coefficients.
Important qualification
A d-dimensional state system supplies a recurrence of order at most d. Its minimal recurrence
need not have order d.
The original statement is read as the characteristic-polynomial
recurrence, not a minimal-order claim. The formal equations also handle singular matrices,
zero-dimensional spaces and zero step.
1. Actual matrix orbits and arithmetic jumps
Theorem 9.1; manuscript page 22
Let R be a commutative ring, I a finite coordinate type and d = |I|. For C ∈MI(R) and
v0 ∈RI, define
orbit(C, v0, n) = Cnv0,
uk = packet(C, v0, N, j, k) = CNk+jv0.
Komorebi P.
179


Euler congruences / Paper IX
Final strengthened edition
The implementation uses actual matrix powers and matrix–vector multiplication. A separately
supplied sequence v with
vn+1 = Cvn
is proved by induction to equal this power orbit. Thus the source-sequence formulation is
connected to the construction.
The jump formula
Associativity and the laws for powers of one matrix give
uk = Cj(CN)kv0 = (CN)k(Cjv0).
Only powers of the same matrix are interchanged. The argument does not assume that
arbitrary matrices commute. In particular,
uk+1 = CNuk,
uk+a = (CN)auk.
The step matrix is CN, not C. A checked example with the Fibonacci matrix and N = 2
rejects replacing the jump with a single original step.
Refinement of an arithmetic address
Taking another subsequence with step M and offset s yields
uMk+s = v(NM)k+(Ns+j).
The formal proof establishes this identity for the actual packet function by checking the index
arithmetic. It is not inferred from a diagram or a naming convention.
Endpoints and interpretation
The algebraic identities allow N = 0, in which case uk = vj is constant. Positive-modulus
residue packets are the special case N > 0 and 0 ≤j < N. The formula also permits offsets
beyond that standard range. No inverse matrix, diagonalization, eigenvalue, or characteristic-
zero assumption is needed.
Principal targets.
Companion.orbit_of_recurrence
Companion.companion_jump,Companion.source_companion_jump
Companion.packet_step,Companion.packet_advance
Companion.packet_refinement,Companion.packet_zero_step

Here χD[a] denotes the coefficient at index a. The identities hold over any commutative

The complete vector recurrence
Apply the matrix identity to the actual state uk. The already proved advance identity identifies
each Dauk with uk+a. Consequently,

supplied by χD. It is not an assumption on the packet sequence.

A separate source-sequence theorem transports the recurrence back to the original vNk+j
notation.
Order versus minimal order
The characteristic polynomial gives a recurrence of order at most d. For example, C = I2 gives
a degree-two characteristic polynomial, but every orbit is constant and obeys uk+1 = uk. Both
statements are checked. No minimal-polynomial classification is claimed.
For d = 0, the unique empty vector is zero and the formula remains valid. For singular
matrices, Cayley–Hamilton still applies: invertibility is irrelevant. The nilpotent example
C = ( 0 1
0 0 ) has C2 = 0 and χC = X2.

Ln+2 = Ln + Ln+1.
Direct finite matrix arithmetic proves C2 = C + I. Multiplication by Cn gives Cn+2 =
Cn + Cn+1. Taking traces shows that tr(Cn) satisfies the same recurrence and initial values as
Ln. Induction yields
tr(CN) = LN.
The implementation separately proves
det C = −1,
det(CN) = (−1)N,
LN = 2FN+1 −FN.
No Binet roots or diagonalizability argument is needed.
The sampled Fibonacci recurrence
The actual two-by-two characteristic-polynomial formula gives
χCN (X) = X2 −LNX + (−1)N.
Insert these coefficients into the general packet recurrence and take the second coordinate:
FN(k+2)+j = LNFN(k+1)+j −(−1)NFNk+j.
The full two-coordinate state satisfies the same equation. All subtraction is in Z, not truncated
natural-number subtraction. The result holds for all natural N, j, k, including zero step and
zero initial values.
Principal targets.
FibonacciPackets.state_is_orbit,FibonacciPackets.packet_is_fibonacci
FibonacciPackets.trace_power_is_lucas,FibonacciPackets.det_power
FibonacciPackets.jump_charpoly
FibonacciPackets.fibonacci_packet_recurrence
4. Exact examples and meaningful boundary checks
Example 9.3 and supporting results
The independently defined Lucas recurrence gives
(L0, L1, L2, L3, L4, L5) = (2, 1, 3, 4, 7, 11).
For N = 3, the general theorem therefore specializes to
uk+2 = 4uk+1 + uk.
The implementation proves this for every offset j and every k, not only the displayed sample.
At j = 2, direct evaluation of the actual Fibonacci sequence gives
(F2, F5, F8, F11, F14, F17) = (1, 5, 21, 89, 377, 1597).
This is the exact manuscript example.
Komorebi P.
182


Euler congruences / Paper IX
Final strengthened edition
The parity sign cannot be removed
For N = 2, the recurrence is
uk+2 = 3uk+1 −uk.
At j = 1, k = 0, the correct equation is 5 = 3 · 2 −1. Replacing the minus sign with a plus
would assert 5 = 7. The source proves the inequality, and a separate invalid-proof control
attempts and rejects the false equation.
At N = 1 the ordinary Fibonacci recurrence is recovered. At N = 0, L0 = 2 and the packet
is constant, so the same formula becomes u = 2u −u.
Uniqueness from the first two packet values
For a fixed N, j, any integer sequence satisfying the proved sampled recurrence and starting at
Fj, FN+j agrees with FNk+j for all k. This is proved by two-step induction. It is a consequence
of the established coefficients and recurrence, not a premise used to identify Fibonacci values
initially.
Independent finite mirrors
A pure-Python exact-arithmetic implementation computes characteristic polynomials by the
permutation determinant and states by repeated matrix application. It checks 280 matrix
systems over integers, rationals and several modular rings, including composite moduli and
the zero ring. Separately it computes sampled Fibonacci sequences recursively and compares
them with direct Fibonacci evaluation. These are useful regression checks, not Lean extraction
or universal proofs.
Principal targets.
RecurrenceExamples.manuscript_sequence

The independent route
In characteristic p, both the first and pth coefficient shifts obey a Leibniz rule for Hurwitz
convolution. A section supported at multiples of m, with m | p −1, has equal first and pth
shifts. Its constructed reciprocal inherits this equality. Identifying that reciprocal with the
existing reduced Euler sequence gives its period on the positive-index tail.
What remains
Theorem 7.5 asks for powers as high as pmin(k,mn) and covers nonstandard residue classes. A
period modulo p does not establish that stronger result. This release proves a genuine standard-
window mod-p fragment and precise conditional interfaces for the remaining argument, while
keeping item 7.5 pending.

The prime-shift cancellation
For prime p and characteristic p, every interior binomial coefficient in row p vanishes. Hence
Up(f ⋆g) = (Upf) ⋆g + f ⋆(Upg).
The first-shift rule has exactly the same form. Both are checked for the literal coefficient
product.

and proves f ⋆g = 1. It also proves cancellation: if a ⋆g = 0, then a = 0, by induction on the
first nonzero coefficient.
If Upg = Ug, differentiate the inverse equation with both operators and subtract. The
terms involving f ⋆(Ug) cancel, leaving
(Upf −Uf) ⋆g = 0.
The proved cancellation law yields
Upf = Uf.
The section identity is an explicit premise of this general reciprocal lemma; the next page
proves that premise for the specific section used by the Euler sequence.

Three checked boundaries
The initial row. For (p, m) = (3, 2), the same reduced recurrence gives e0 = 1 and e1 = −1 = 2
in F3. Its proposed period is one, so the positive-tail result cannot in general include row zero.
The divisor hypothesis. At (p, m) = (5, 3), where 3 ∤4, the independently computed
reduced row is

All orders, without a circular closure assumption
Let I ⊂A be a subring stable under U. Define
Boundedr(f) ⇐⇒Bsf ∈I (0 ≤s ≤r),
Flat(f) ⇐⇒Bsf ∈I (s ≥0).
The definitions contain no product or inverse closure requirement. Since B commutes with U,
derivatives of a bounded element remain bounded. Induction on r in the displayed product
rule then proves closure under multiplication. The finite middle sums use the lower-order
induction hypothesis, not the desired next-order conclusion.
For an actual inverse fg = 1, applying the product rule and multiplying by g gives
B(g) = −g2B(f) −gMp2(f, g) + gMp(f, g).
If g ∈I, induction on r proves boundedness of g from boundedness of f. At each successor
stage the preceding stage supplies boundedness of g needed on the right. Passing to all finite
orders proves reciprocal closure for an integral inverse.

The standard interval without an analytic power-series-in-the-index premise
On Hurwitz coefficients, the actual Euler operator plus c is

the proposed preimage, and does not assert invertibility at a singular pivot p | c.
